**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Pate* and *Drope* require meaningful retrospective evaluation rather than presumed reconstruction. *Godinez* preserves functional capacity, renewed inquiry, and the distinction from knowing and voluntary waiver. *Riggins*'s supported medication-related impairment does not establish that reconstruction is impossible here; *Tuggle* supplies no automatic retrial or universal error rule.

### Postverdict acquittal and corrective authority

#### Carlisle v. United States, 517 U.S. 416

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-9247.
**Decided:** April 29, 1996.

##### Authority to grant a new, untimely postverdict acquittal

**Holding:** With the Government's timely objection preserved, Rules 29(c) and 45(b) forbid granting a new acquittal motion filed outside the seven-day period following jury discharge, absent further time fixed within that period under the then-operative rule. Rule 45(b) excludes ordinary excusable-neglect enlargement; this limits the motion remedy, not federal criminal subject-matter jurisdiction. Rule 2, Rule 57, and inherent supervisory authority cannot supply the same prohibited relief under a separate pre-sentence-power label.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Scalia), Parts I–II, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; seven Justices.

**Material application:** No timely trial motion, reserved decision, or valid extension existed. The seven-day computation excluded intervening weekends; the filing was one day late. Attorney error, absence of prejudice, and later reconsideration do not create authority. Rule 29(a)'s acquittal duty cannot nullify the specific postverdict conditions. Rule 2 guides construction, and Rule 57 permits consistent practice in genuine gaps; neither overrides a national rule. Amended Rule 57's nonwillful local-form and actual-notice protections do not excuse this national deadline, and its just-and-practicable pending-proceedings transition does not automatically apply it to the 1993 conduct.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Inherent authority is not abolished generally. Timely court-initiated action, reconsideration of a timely motion, a valid reservation, Government forfeiture, materially different judicial assurances inducing lateness, and hypothetical Article III withdrawal limits remain open. No sufficiency determination follows.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Smith* supports the restriction against an unrestricted judicial substitute while retaining its different postappeal posture. *Pioneer* permits flexibility only where enlargement is authorized. *Bank of Nova Scotia* requires rule-consistent supervision; *Williams*'s bounded, prejudice-based remedy survives without authorizing a conflicting act. *Fallen* protects timely efforts of an ill, effectively unrepresented prisoner, not ordinary counsel error. *Lonchar* preserves process actually required by law without an additional equitable substitute. *Manypenny*'s timely-motion reconsideration does not resolve the new late motion.

##### Writs, due process, and restoration of the verdict

**Holding:** Neither the All Writs Act nor due process authorizes this untimely acquittal on the presented facts. The Government may obtain restoration of the existing jury verdict without a second factfinding trial. Independently authorized review remains subject to its own preservation, proof, and procedural conditions; the Court neither adjudicates nor guarantees such relief.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part III of the same seven-Justice Court opinion.

**Material application:** Section 1651's necessary-or-appropriate, in-aid-of-jurisdiction authority consistent with legal usages supplies no exceptional writ predicate here. No predeadline judicial assurance induced lateness. Substantive protection against insufficient evidence does not create an additional late district-court vehicle; a missed motion is not necessarily an informed waiver of every other claim, and plain-error review is not declared equivalent to ordinary sufficiency review.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the no-authority ruling, 7–2; the late acquittal remains unauthorized. The reinstated conviction and sentence already imposed remain undisturbed. No duplicate sentencing, trial, release, custody determination, new collateral proceeding, or reopened claim follows. Any independently available collateral proceeding must satisfy genuinely applicable AEDPA conditions and unresolved transition requirements; none is decided applicable or satisfied here.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Morgan*'s extraordinary coram nobis remedy is not another deadline for this ongoing prosecution. *Jackson*'s sufficiency protection survives; *Wilson* permits the Government appeal because restoration requires no new trial. *Harris Truck Lines* and *Thompson* supply no missing judicial-assurance predicate. *Olano* and *Libretti* retain distinct authority, forfeiture, waiver, and lawful-correction inquiries.

### Reasonable-doubt instructions and expert evidence

#### Felker v. Turpin, 518 U.S. 651 (1996)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 95-8836; associated application A-890.
**Decided:** June 28, 1996.

##### Whether the complete reasonable-doubt charge and voir-dire statements permit conviction on a lesser standard

**Holding:** The challenged voir-dire statements and complete final charge do not create a reasonable likelihood that the jury convicted without proof beyond a reasonable doubt of every essential element. The presented constitutional instruction ground therefore supplies no habeas relief.

**Proposition-level authority:** O’Connor’s Part III-A, all eight Associate Justices; Stone-Zsela joins denial on narrower successive/extraordinary grounds.

**Material application:** The charge maintained the State’s burden, distinguished criminal proof from civil preponderance, linked doubt to evidence or its lack/conflict, required acquittal when wavering, unsettled or unsatisfied, required exclusion of other reasonable theories for entirely circumstantial proof, and rejected suspicion, conjecture and possibility. Those connected instructions and treatment of counsel’s statements defeat the proposed lesser moral-impression interpretation.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Correct boilerplate does not automatically cure contradiction. No new instruction, waiver or retroactivity issue is decided; prosecution strength is not used as harmless-error substitution.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Victor/Sandoval’s whole-charge reasonable-likelihood rule is applied. Sullivan’s structural rule remains; the constitutional predicate for it is absent.

##### Whether the medical testimony and later criticism establish fundamental trial unfairness

**Holding:** On this record, admission of Tillman's testimony and the submitted forensic criticisms do not establish constitutionally fundamental unfairness or the requested constitutional rule that only a physician may provide such testimony. This is a determination of the presented trial-process claim, not a finding that each challenged medical conclusion was correct.

**Proposition-level authority:** O’Connor’s Part III-B, all eight Associate Justices; Stone-Zsela relies on a distinct extraordinary-review ground.

**Material application:** Credentials were exposed in examination, contrary testimony was available, Whitaker gave independent testimony, and the charge reserved expert weight and factual assumptions to jurors. Later detailed criticism is considered, not dismissed as credentials-only disagreement, but does not establish the asserted constitutional violation.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No finding validates every forensic conclusion or establishes perjury, knowing false-evidence use, concealed qualifications or deprivation of a required expert. No supporting observation is converted into a new claim.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Lisenba supplies fundamental fairness. Ake, Ford and Eddings supply no automatic physician-only testimony rule. For original jurisdiction, successive screening and the distinct innocence gateway, see Federal Courts — Successive habeas petitions and original jurisdiction, Felker, decided June 28, 1996, No. 95-8836.

### Rebuttal of retained mental-state elements

#### Montana v. Egelhoff, 518 U.S. 37

**Docket or dockets:** 95-566
**Decided:** June 13, 1996

##### May an appellate court substitute a sober-person culpability rule for the State's retained element?

**Holding:** The constitutional inquiry must address purpose or knowledge actually retained by state law, not a substituted offense treating the defendant as sober. Montana's highest court construed deliberate homicide as retaining that element and §45-2-203 as restricting evidence; that authoritative state-law premise controls.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (O'Connor), joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter and Breyer; five direct joins on all holdings.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court does not require subjective intent in every offense or decide the validity, notice or retroactivity of a genuinely different culpability definition, including an express intoxication-as-knowledge law.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Patterson preserves genuine offense definition and separate affirmative-defense allocations, not appellate replacement of the retained offense. Gaudin requires jury determination of actual enacted elements. Ratzlaf and Staples remain interpretations of particular federal crimes.

##### May Montana exclude otherwise admissible intoxication evidence from consideration of purpose or knowledge?

**Holding:** Due process forbids applying §45-2-203 to prevent consideration of otherwise admissible intoxication evidence bearing on the retained purpose-or-knowledge element without sufficient justification. Ordinary rules of relevance, reliability, privilege and fair trial management survive; there is no absolute evidence right or affirmative intoxication excuse.

**Proposition-level authority:** The same five-Justice opinion.

**Material application:** Admission for memory or physical-capacity purposes did not cure the instruction forbidding mental-state use. Historical restrictions, responsibility and deterrence do not sufficiently justify excluding rebuttal of the element retained here. Blackout or high intoxication does not establish absence of purpose, and directed physical conduct does not conclusively establish it.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Section 45-2-203's unknown-intoxicant exception requires the defendant to prove he did not know the substance was intoxicating when ingested by consuming, smoking, sniffing, injecting or otherwise ingesting it. It is not a general exception for involuntary intoxication, unexpected severity or lost memory. No finding that Egelhoff satisfies it or separate ruling on its persuasion burden is made.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Montana Supreme Court affirmed, 5–4; its new-trial judgment on both homicide counts stands. Otherwise admissible intoxication evidence must be usable on purpose or knowledge. No precise impairment, insufficiency or harmlessness finding, acquittal, release or determination of the remaining lay-opinion, sufficiency and dangerous-offender assignments follows.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Winship protects proof beyond reasonable doubt of actual elements. Martin distinguishes a separate defense burden from evidence remaining usable against the prosecution's elements. Chambers and Rock protect a meaningful defense subject to justified evidence limits. Victor's whole-charge review does not make general burden language cure an express contrary restriction. Medina's California competency allocation and Cooper's higher-burden prohibition remain distinct. Sullivan's structural-error rule is not extended to every defense-evidence restriction.

### Selective prosecution and discovery

#### United States v. Armstrong, 517 U.S. 456

**Docket or dockets:** No. 95-157.
**Decided:** May 13, 1996.

##### Rule 16 authority for selective-prosecution discovery

**Holding:** Rule 16(a)(1)(C)'s defense-preparation materiality provision does not authorize discovery to prepare this selective-prosecution challenge to the decision to prosecute. The holding concerns that claim alone, rather than all affirmative defenses unrelated to guilt or every possible source of constitutionally warranted inquiry.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (O'Connor), Part I, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; six. Souter and Ginsburg join only the stated selective-prosecution scope. Breyer supplies a different source analysis; Stone-Zsela does not join Part I. Stevens's separate agreement that the Rule supplies no entitlement is not a join in the Court's reasoning.

**Material application:** The other disjunctive triggers—Government intent to use the material in its evidence in chief and acquisition from or ownership by the defendant—are not established. Protective and sanction powers regulate an existing duty rather than create it. No established entitlement under Rule 17(c), *Brady*, or compulsory process supplies the missing source on this showing. The Court makes no waiver finding and does not attribute its construction to the Government or lower courts.

##### Preliminary showing of discriminatory selection

**Holding:** Discovery on this indirect racial-selection theory requires some credible evidence tending to show discriminatory effect and purpose, including a reliable basis to infer that relevantly similarly situated persons of another race eligible for federal prosecution could have been prosecuted but were not. This is less than a prima facie merits case. Grounded statistics, circumstantial comparisons, and a specifically justified alternative evidentiary basis when comparator names cannot reasonably be obtained may establish the inference; neither perfect identity, universal naming, nor a fixed checklist is required.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II, joined by Stone-Zsela, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; eight. The shared rule assumes discovery may be available on an appropriate showing without establishing another source of authority.

**Material application:** The selected sample of 24 Black federal defendants supplies a numerator, not an eligible charging population or sufficiently comparable offenders passed over. State-practice experience and relayed treatment observations, considered cumulatively with the developed Government response, fail to connect the comparison to relevant quantities, firearms, evidence, and criminal histories. Government proffers identify gaps without establishing neutrality or disproving the sample's bounded count. Insufficiency does not prove comparators nonexistent. Access difficulty is real; discovery may complete a grounded inference but cannot create its missing connection solely from hope.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The request fails on discriminatory effect; no independent purpose deficiency or ultimate discrimination finding is decided. Whether direct discriminatory admissions dispense with similarly situated comparison remains open. No completed-merits burden, categorical direct-evidence rule, universally available comparator database, guaranteed state-record access, or enforceable subpoena access is presumed.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Wayte* supplies effect-and-purpose limits but reserved discovery. *Wade* supplies meaningful preliminary screening by analogy from sentencing, not an already controlling charging rule. *Kimberlin* preserves statistical and circumstantial proof alongside lawful limits; *Klinger* ties similarity to the challenged treatment without requiring identity. *McCleskey* requires connection to the challenged decision without categorically excluding statistics or requiring merits proof for discovery. *Yick Wo*'s supported-pattern inference remains available; this record lacks its comparison connection.

##### Consequences for the order, dependent dismissal, and remand

**Holding:** The unsupported discovery order and dismissal for refusing it cannot stand. The case returns for ordinary pretrial proceedings without a directed renewed inquiry or automatic discovery; a later lawful, adequately supported request remains possible under its own source, threshold, and protective conditions.

**Proposition-level authority:** Setting aside the existing order and dismissal has the eight-Justice Part II coalition. Part III's formal reversal and ordinary remand has O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; seven. Stone-Zsela would expressly vacate and direct conditional staged inquiry; Stevens would affirm. Those positions do not alter the controlling mandate.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the Ninth Circuit and remand, 7–2; the setting-aside component is 8–1. No new evidence-gathering opportunity, guilt determination, conviction, sentence, state-prosecution order, acquittal, or retrial after acquittal is directed. The Court assigns no dismissal-prejudice label.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Appropriateness of the sanction on the assumption of a valid discovery order and the remedy for proved selective prosecution are not decided.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Williams*'s grand-jury duty, prejudice requirements, and without-prejudice remedy do not automatically govern this distinct sanction. *Carlisle* preserves rule-consistent supervision without converting Rule 16 nonauthorization into universal immunity from constitutional review.

### Bank false statements and statutory elements

#### United States v. Wells

**Docket or dockets:** No. 95-1228.
**Decided:** February 26, 1997.

For the separate questions in United States v. Wells, No. 95-1228 decided February 26, 1997, see Federal Courts — Preservation and questions passed upon below (The Court may review the statutory question actually decided below).

##### Section 1014 does not add an objective materiality element

**Holding:** Section 1014 does not require an additional finding that a statement had a natural tendency or objective capacity to influence the institution's decision. The Government must still prove beyond a reasonable doubt the applicable conduct—knowingly making a false statement or report, or willfully overvaluing land, property or security—and the purpose of influencing an institution enumerated by the statute in a covered transaction.

The covered transactions are an application, advance, discount, purchase, purchase agreement, repurchase agreement, commitment or loan; a change or extension of one through renewal, deferment or otherwise; or acceptance, release or substitution of its security. The institutional requirement includes the FDIC-insured institutions relevant here; it is not displaced by calling a transaction financial.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II of Thomas's opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer; eight Justices. Stevens dissents from this rule and vacatur.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Purpose to influence is distinct from objective materiality and remains an element alongside the applicable knowing-false-statement or willful-overvaluation conduct and covered institution and transaction. Prosecutorial discretion, likely consequential lies and sentencing do not replace proof. No general materiality ruling for §1001 or every fraud statute follows.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The two-conviction appellate judgment is vacated 8–1. Part III's limits command all nine votes: acquittals cannot be reopened; remaining indictment, instruction, evidence, conspiracy and sentence grounds require their own availability and merits determinations. Neither a new trial, restored conviction or sentence nor harmlessness follows automatically.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Kay rejects actual-influence defenses within its predecessor setting; Williams preserves its conclusion that the checks were not factual assertions. Gaudin requires juries to decide actual offense elements but creates none. Roy distinguishes permissible error review from an established harmlessness result and does not automatically govern this different direct appeal.

### Prior-conviction status evidence

#### Old Chief v. United States

**Docket or dockets:** No. 95-6556.

**Decided:** January 7, 1997.

##### An adequate status admission must enter the Rule 403 comparison

**Holding:** When an actually available, unconditional and legally adequate admission establishes the complete prior-conviction status element, that element is the Government's sole legitimate purpose for the evidence, and the name or nature of the conviction presents unfair propensity danger substantially outweighing its incremental legitimate probative value, Rule 403 requires exclusion of that name or nature. On this record, admitting the similar-assault judgment after rejecting the offered substitute on general prosecutorial-choice grounds was an abuse of discretion; the Ninth Circuit's no-error judgment is reversed.

The admission must establish a conviction qualifying under §922(g)(1) and §921(a)(20). The latter excludes Federal or State antitrust, unfair-trade, restraint-of-trade and similar business-regulation offenses, and State offenses classified as misdemeanors and punishable by two years or less. Conviction status follows the law of the jurisdiction of the proceedings. Expunged or set-aside convictions, pardoned convictions and convictions for which civil rights have been restored do not count, subject to the statute's qualification when the pardon, expungement or restoration expressly prohibits shipping, transporting, possessing or receiving firearms. No such exclusion is found applicable here; the offered instruction requires appropriate consensual clarification rather than an assumption that its original wording was flawless.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Part I, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter and Breyer; six Justices.

**Limits and questions not reached:** A distinct lawful evidentiary purpose requires its own showing and balance. Partial or conditional admissions need not be accepted; an adequate sanitized judgment may suffice. The preserved judgment-record objection was not forfeited by the separate credibility-instruction history. Legal adequacy of the status admission requires consensual clarification; the status element remains for adequate evidence and jury instructions.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Huddleston's safeguards are applied to comparative status proof. Gaudin's requirement that the element be established remains intact; no general compulsory-stipulation doctrine is adopted.

##### Harmlessness remains a separate whole-record inquiry

**Holding:** The established preserved Rule 403 error is subject to Rule 52(a) and Kotteakos: the Government must establish, from the whole record, fair assurance that the error did not substantially sway the judgment, and grave doubt defeats harmlessness. The case is remanded for that inquiry and lawful consequences for affected convictions; a new trial follows only if error requiring relief is established, without a present finding that every count was affected.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Part II, joined by Stevens, Kennedy, Souter and Breyer; five Justices. Stone-Zsela joins the error ruling and reversal but opposes leaving harmlessness open.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The error is nonconstitutional, so Chapman and Roy do not replace Kotteakos; Olano's ordinary defendant burden concerns forfeited error. No present harmlessness, acquittal, automatic retrial or count-specific outcome is found.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Williamson's preserved nonconstitutional-error rule is applied unchanged; Kotteakos supplies the substantial-influence inquiry, Olano is distinguished at preservation, and Chapman and Roy remain confined to the constitutional errors they govern.

### Accepted pleas and deferred plea agreements

#### United States v. Hyde

**Docket or dockets:** No. 96-667.
**Decided:** 1997-05-27.

##### An accepted plea requires a fair and just reason for presentence withdrawal

**Holding:** When a district court has accepted a guilty plea following the required Rule 11 inquiry but has deferred acceptance of the associated plea agreement, former Rule 32(e) requires a fair and just reason to withdraw the plea before sentencing. Acceptance of the plea and acceptance of the agreement are distinct acts; deferral of the latter does not create an unconditional right to undo the former.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (O'Connor), unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** This is a former Rule 11(e)(1)(A) agreement under which the Government moves to dismiss charges. For an A agreement or an (e)(1)(C) agreement specifying a sentence, rejection triggers Rule 11(e)(4): the court must disclose the rejection, personally advise that it is not bound, afford the defendant an opportunity to withdraw without a fair-and-just-reason showing, and warn that persisting may produce a less favorable disposition. A type B sentencing recommendation is different: the defendant must be warned that failure to follow the recommendation does not itself give a withdrawal right. Deferral is not rejection under either category. Actual acceptance, the agreement's lawful terms, knowing and voluntary assent, and supported coercion or other fair-and-just claims remain independently relevant. Former Rule 11(f)'s factual-basis prerequisite attaches to entry of judgment on the accepted plea; this record's earlier inquiry is not a universal new acceptance sequence. The Government's obligation before agreement acceptance is not decided, and the ruling authorizes no prosecutorial retraction.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the automatic-withdrawal rule and remand, 9–0, leaving properly preserved factual and remaining challenges under the correct rules. No new finding of duress, voluntariness or factual basis, automatic rehearing, sentence or trial is directed.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Kercheval supplies the fair-and-just background. Santobello and Mabry preserve actual inducing promises and valid assent without merging the two acceptance acts. Godinez separates competence from valid relinquishment; Mezzanatto supplies no inferred advance waiver, and Libretti preserves consent and statutory punishment limits.

### Criminal civil-rights liability and fair warning

#### United States v. Lanier

**Docket or dockets:** No. 95-1717.
**Decided:** March 31, 1997.

##### What law must supply fair warning under 18 U.S.C. §242

**Holding:** Section 242, read with authoritative constructions, must have made the particular constitutional unlawfulness reasonably apparent when the defendant acted. Neither a later decision nor willful bad purpose can supply missing conduct-time notice, and novel or unforeseeable criminal enlargement is forbidden.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Stone-Zsela), unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Moral reprehensibility and state criminality do not replace a definite federal right and notice. Vagueness, lenity, and the bar on unexpected retrospective enlargement retain related but distinct functions.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Screws* supplies definite-right and separate willfulness requirements; courts construe the criminal statute rather than create common-law offenses.

##### Whether notice requires a Supreme Court decision on fundamentally similar facts

**Holding:** Fair warning may come from relevant pre-existing lower-court authority and from an obviously covered application of an established rule without a factual twin. Neither a Supreme-Court-only requirement nor a fundamentally-similar-facts prerequisite is lawful; conversely, an abstract liberty or bodily-security proposition does not settle an uncertain application.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Stone-Zsela), unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** One lower-court decision or a fixed numerical consensus is not automatically sufficient. Actual conflicts, authority, particular context, and express reservations matter. No count's constitutional basis, notice, personal responsibility, or official nexus is resolved.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Elder* supplies examination of all relevant existing authority. *Anderson* requires particularized apparent unlawfulness, not repeated factual identity. *Pinder* and *Uhlrig* preserve unsettled-application and attribution limits; later holdings cannot supply earlier notice.

##### Whether the civil clarity standard substitutes for criminal elements

**Holding:** Criminal fair warning under §242 and civil clearly-established-law analysis use the same legal-clarity threshold: pre-existing law must make the constitutional unlawfulness apparent. The common threshold does not displace proof of deprivation, action under color of law, criminal willfulness, the criminal burden of proof, or any other statutory element.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Stone-Zsela), unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Civil immunity analysis cannot itself convict, and judicial office alone does not prove that every act occurred under color of law. No categorically stricter criminal clarity standard is adopted.

##### Whether due-process rights or direct official assaults outside custody are categorically excluded

**Holding:** Appellate review may not exclude due-process rights from §242 categorically or treat the victim's lack of custody as automatic immunity for an official's own assault. Where a specific constitutional provision covers the claim, that provision governs; the Fourth and Eighth Amendments do not exhaust every potentially covered abusive official act.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Stone-Zsela), unanimous.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Vacate the en banc judgment and remand for count-specific consideration under the proper conduct-time notice rule and all remaining properly presented issues. The mandate does not reinstate convictions or sentence, order acquittal or retrial, or require a new evidentiary hearing.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No universal constitutional-assault formula or factual assault finding is made. Constitutional wrongdoing, personal participation, omitted protection, and supervisory attribution remain distinct.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Screws* and *Price* preserve §242's due-process reach. *DeShaney*'s private-violence rule does not immunize direct official wrongdoing. *Graham* supplies the specific-provision rule. *Collins*, *Doe v. Taylor*, *Pinder*, *Uhlrig*, and *Nabozny* retain their distinct deprivation and attribution boundaries, without supplying notice retrospectively.

### Electronic obscenity transportation and venue

#### United States v. Thomas

**Docket or dockets:** Sixth Circuit Nos. 94-6648 and 94-6649; no separate Supreme Court docket assigned.
**Decided:** 1997-05-19.

**Companion or consolidated matters:** Robert and Carleen Thomas's appeals are consolidated. For the separate community-standards holding, see First Amendment — Electronic obscenity distribution, United States v. Thomas, May 19, 1997, these dockets.

##### The offense-date statute covers these transmitted images

**Holding:** Offense-date §1465 reaches obscene visual image files knowingly transported through interstate computer-and-telephone facilities for sale or distribution. Applying its picture, image and electrical-transcription terms to this means of conveying the charged images does not depend on a later computer-specific amendment; the statutory distribution purpose and applicable culpability requirements remain necessary.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Part I, unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The distribution purpose and applicable culpability remain necessary; no rule brings every new technology within every old crime.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Alpers applies comprehensive media terms to different means of actual conveyance; purpose alone creates no offense. Lanier requires conduct-time fair warning, not a retroactive computer-specific amendment or decision.

##### Venue lies where the authorized interstate transportation ends

**Holding:** Article III, the Sixth Amendment and 18 U.S.C. §3237(a) permit trial in the district where the charged continuing interstate transportation reaches the recipient whom the distributor authorized to receive it. The approved Memphis downloads completed that transportation there; known destination supports this application without becoming a universal additional statutory element.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Part III, unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The ruling concerns actual authorized delivery, not remote effects alone, every district crossed by signals, unknown viewers or passive pages. Knowledge of destination supports this application without becoming a universal added statutory element.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the reviewed convictions on statutory coverage, receiving-community standards and venue, 8–1. Robert Thomas's Count 11 acquittal is untouched; entrapment, selective prosecution, evidence, counsel, sentencing and forfeiture remain outside decision.

### Plain error after intervening law

#### Johnson v. United States

**Docket or dockets:** No. 96-203.
**Decided:** 1997-05-12.

##### The jury-element violation is reviewed as forfeited error

**Holding:** Materiality is an element of the §1623 perjury offense and must be determined by the jury beyond a reasonable doubt; the judge's determination instead was constitutional error. Johnson's failure to object presents forfeiture under Rule 52(b), not an established intentional waiver extinguishing that error.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone-Zsela's opinion, unanimous in Parts I and IV.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Gaudin supplies the jury obligation for actual materiality elements and Griffith applies it on pending direct review. Wells's different §1014 construction does not eliminate §1623 materiality; Olano separates forfeiture from intentional waiver.

##### Plainness is measured at appellate consideration in this intervening-law setting

**Holding:** When the law at trial settled an issue against the defendant but controlling law changes before direct review ends, the error is plain if it is clear at appellate consideration. This resolves that intervening-law setting without deciding every case in which the law was unsettled at trial or dispensing with Rule 52(b)'s other requirements.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone-Zsela's Part II, joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; eight direct joins. Scalia assumes plainness without joining.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Olano's reservation about error first clarified on appeal is resolved for settled contrary trial law changed before direct review ends, without resolving initially unsettled law.

##### The independent discretionary condition does not warrant correction here

**Holding:** Even assuming an effect on substantial rights, this forfeited materiality error does not warrant correction under Rule 52(b) because, in the established circumstances, it did not seriously affect the fairness, integrity or public reputation of the proceedings. That conclusion rests on the clear materiality of the source-of-funds account in the particular investigation and the intervening change from settled trial law; it is neither a general overwhelming-evidence exception nor a substitute jury verdict.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone-Zsela's opinion, unanimous in Parts I and IV.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Substantial-rights impact is assumed; structural classification and ordinary third-step prejudice remain open. There is no innocence requirement or need for a second institutional wrong, no substitute element finding, and no universal overwhelming-evidence exception. Disputed materiality, less decisive proof or trial-strategy effects remain open.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the conviction-sustaining judgment, 9–0, without a new trial, acquittal or sentence calculation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Olano's fourth condition independently governs correction after forfeiture. Gaudin's classification reservation, Sullivan's vitiated-reasonable-doubt rule and Roy's distinct preserved personal-intent habeas inquiry remain intact.

### Retrospective punishment and civil commitment

#### Kansas v. Hendricks; Hendricks v. Kansas

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 95-1649 and 95-9075.
**Decided:** June 23, 1997.
**Companion or consolidated matters:** Consolidated cross-petitions concern the same commitment and receive one affirmance on the as-applied Ex Post Facto ground.

##### The civil designation of personal commitment remains rebuttable by clearest proof of punishment

**Holding:** In assessing whether Kansas's civil commitment of Hendricks constitutes punishment for Ex Post Facto purposes, the Court first determines the Legislature's intended civil or criminal character; an intended civil scheme is treated as punishment only upon the clearest proof that its purpose or effect is punitive. The Act's civil designation is relevant but not conclusive, and neither confinement, a connection to crime, indeterminate duration, criminal-style procedural protection nor treatment difficulty alone decides that demanding inquiry.

**Proposition-level authority:** Breyer's Parts II–III, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg: five Justices.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Statutory care, review and release obligations are relevant but do not prove actual compliance. Clearest proof does not demand successful cure or make each treatment deficiency punishment; no burden shift, mandatory alternatives inquiry or exhaustive checklist is created.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Ursery’s civil-intent and clearest-proof method is extended from its specified forfeitures to this personal-confinement question, without transferring its forfeiture outcome or Double Jeopardy limits. Ward preserves demanding intent-and-effect analysis; Mendoza-Martinez permits cumulative assessment without a conclusive single indicator.

##### The cumulative original operation makes this retrospective commitment punishment

**Holding:** The cumulative circumstances of Hendricks's original commitment—additional confinement after his sentence, selection tied to earlier sexual offenses, prisonlike restraint, and initially inadequate treatment sustained on the prospect of a future specialized program despite acknowledged possible treatment—provide the clearest proof of punitive operation in this application. Because the Act imposes that additional punishment for conduct predating its enactment, its application to Hendricks violates the Ex Post Facto Clause, and the judgment invalidating this commitment is affirmed.

**Proposition-level authority:** Breyer's Parts II–III, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg: five Justices.

**Material application:** At the October 1994 hearing, specialist contracts remained unsigned and the dedicated program was not established; commitment nevertheless continued on the prospect of a future program. Procurement efforts, direct-care staff, general hospital professionals, planned evaluation and an earlier prison-treatment offer remain part of the contrary evidence. The cumulative conclusion does not rest on total absence of clinicians or treatment opportunities.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No failed-bid motive, deliberate withholding of effective therapy, complete absence of a clinical plan, rejected adequate alternative, sham release process or later treatment regimen is found. No prospective facial invalidity, automatic rule for later genuinely civil programs, or validity of another commitment route is decided. Double Jeopardy is not reached. The non-dispositive due-process discussion supplies no holding on diagnostic vocabulary, treatment-related process, broader propensity definitions, personality disorder, absolute inability to control conduct or a new instruction.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the Kansas judgment invalidating this commitment in both dockets, 5–4. Hendricks may not remain confined under this application for pre-enactment conduct. No new factfinding remand, release date, stay, hearing schedule, clinical protocol or alternative custody authorization is imposed.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Sahhar’s rejection of a hypothetical sentence ceiling presupposes independently lawful civil custody and does not classify this Kansas confinement or import federal procedures. Foucha, Addington and Heller are unchanged; non-dispositive discussion creates no new due-process rule. Morales remains confined to its parole-opportunity subject, Lynce supplies exhaustion only, and Blockburger and retained Grady are not applied.

### Direct contempt and habeas review

#### Pounders v. Watson

**Docket or dockets:** No. 96-1383.

**Decided:** June 27, 1997.

##### Whether direct contempt requires physical disruption, pervasive misconduct or demonstrated future repetition

**Holding:** The narrow power to adjudicate direct contempt summarily is not confined to noisy or physical obstruction, a pattern of violations pervading the trial, or a separate finding that misconduct would recur without immediate punishment. The Ninth Circuit's excessive reliance on the limited duration of Watson's questioning and absence of demonstrated future repetition cannot sustain its summary-process ruling on this record; the Court does not finally resolve her unaddressed notice and willfulness claims.

**Proposition-level authority:** Per curiam opinion joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; six direct votes. Stone-Zsela dissents on the merits; Stevens and Breyer oppose summary disposition without deciding the eventual merits.

**Material application:** Renewed punishment questioning after an immediate admonition and the trial judge's deliberate-defiance and jury-prejudice findings support rejection of the appellate process rationale. Those findings do not independently establish the jurors' mental state or finally resolve the reserved notice and willfulness claims.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Essential elements must be observed by the judge and the misconduct must disturb court business and require vindication of judicial authority. Observation, irritation and a brief sanction alone are insufficient; summary punishment for every evidentiary violation is not approved.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse only the summary-process ruling and dependent direction to grant habeas relief; remand for the unresolved notice and willfulness claims. No final denial of habeas relief, renewed confinement, new contempt prosecution or disciplinary result is directed.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Oliver and Cooke retain the narrow exception and ordinary process outside it. Wilson covers nonphysical interference with ongoing trial administration. Sacher permits protective deferral of qualifying punishment. McConnell is distinguished because the threatened questioning there did not occur after the recess. Harris retains ordinary process in its distinct grand-jury setting; federal Rule 42 is an analogy only.

##### How former §2254(d) applies to the unresolved notice and willfulness claims

**Holding:** Former 28 U.S.C. §2254(d) governs this noncapital habeas application filed before AEDPA. Its conditional protection of qualifying state historical findings does not conclusively determine the constitutional legality of summary procedure, and the Ninth Circuit's express nondecision of notice and willfulness does not amount to accepting those findings.

**Proposition-level authority:** Per curiam opinion joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; six direct votes. Stone-Zsela dissents on the merits; Stevens and Breyer oppose summary disposition without deciding the eventual merits.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The former presumption requires a competent state court's factual determination after a merits hearing involving the applicant and State or its agent, evidenced by reliable adequate writing. Its exceptions cover unresolved factual merits, inadequate factfinding procedure, inadequately developed material facts, absent subject-matter or personal jurisdiction, unconstitutional denial of appointed counsel to an indigent, lack of a full and fair adequate hearing, other due-process denial, or lack of fair support in the pertinent state record as a whole. At a federal evidentiary hearing, following due proof of the state determination, convincing evidence of error is required unless one of the first seven circumstances is established, appears or is admitted, or the federal court finds the eighth. Disagreement alone establishes neither an exception nor a right to a hearing.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The notice and willfulness issues expressly reserved below must be considered under this framework. Any established defense requiring facts beyond the judge's observation receives the applicable due-process assessment; no such predicate or judicial bias is found.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Lindh supplies the application-date rule. Sumner preserves the conditional historical-fact presumption. Thompson distinguishes historical circumstances from ultimate constitutional review at its actual scope.

##### Whether the Court must introduce the unestablished Teague defense in this review

**Holding:** The Court declines to introduce a Teague defense that is not established as preserved and fairly included in this review record. That choice is not a finding of categorical waiver, a determination of finality or novelty, or a change to either contemporary Teague exception.

**Proposition-level authority:** Per curiam opinion joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; six direct votes. Stone-Zsela dissents on the merits; Stevens and Breyer oppose summary disposition without deciding the eventual merits.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Schiro permits discretionary refusal of a late nonjurisdictional defense. Caspari's consistently preserved subsidiary-defense premise is not established; neither the contemporary exceptions nor finality and novelty rules are changed.

### Interstate support offenses and temporal limits

#### United States v. Mussari; United States v. Schroeder

**Docket or dockets:** Ninth Circuit Nos. 95-10479 and 95-10513; district cases CR-95-00009-PGR and CR-95-00010-1-PGR. No separate Supreme Court docket number is supplied.

**Decided:** June 30, 1997.

For the interstate-payment power and private-regulation holdings in Mussari/Schroeder, June 30, 1997, Ninth Circuit Nos. 95-10479 and 95-10513, see Constitutional Structure — Interstate support-payment obligations. For the domestic-relations boundary, see Federal Courts — Domestic-relations boundaries.

##### Whether conviction may rest on failures before §228's enactment

**Holding:** Federal conviction and punishment must rest on willful failures after §228's October 25, 1992 enactment, with every applicable element proved beyond a reasonable doubt. Earlier arrears may establish lawful background or past-due status, but a completed pre-enactment failure cannot itself constitute the federal offense.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (O'Connor), Parts I–IV, unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Willfulness, capacity to pay, qualifying amount or duration, necessary interstate residence and sentencing facts remain unproved. Lawful ability-related and other defenses survive. No comprehensive continuing-offense theory dispenses with separate proof or notice questions.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** The Ex Post Facto Clause supplies the independent criminal prohibition. Landgraf and Rivers reinforce attention to consequences of completed conduct but retain their separate civil scope.

##### What each trial remand and any later restitution determination permit

**Holding:** The Ninth Circuit's judgment reversing each district-court indictment dismissal and remanding each prosecution is affirmed, subject to the post-enactment limit and ordinary proof requirements. Section 228(c) requires restitution under §3663 upon conviction equal to the past-due obligation existing at sentencing; this pretrial decision fixes no amount and does not decide the precise lawful treatment of older arrears in such an order.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (O'Connor), Parts I–IV, unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** A first offense permits a fine, up to six months' imprisonment, or both; a subsequent offense permits a fine, up to two years, or both. No prior offense is found. The offense-date limit neither categorically excludes older arrears from eventual restitution nor automatically permits them; that statutory and constitutional issue remains unresolved.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Both indictment dismissals remain reversed and ordinary prosecution may proceed within existing charges and proof limits. No new allegations, conviction, sentence, restitution award, state-order revision or compulsory state administrative act is ordered.

#### United States v. Parker

**Docket or dockets:** Third Circuit No. 95-2018; review of 108 F.3d 28.
**Decided:** December 1, 1997.

##### Which failures to pay can constitute the original §228 offense

**Holding:** Only willful failures after October 25, 1992 may constitute the offense under the original §228. Older arrears may establish background or past-due status, while every actual offense element remains subject to proof beyond a reasonable doubt and lawful ability-related and other defenses remain available.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (O'Connor), unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Reinstatement of the information does not adjudicate proof, guilt, sentence, a restitution amount or whether older arrears may enter an eventual restitution order.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Mussari/Schroeder's post-enactment and actual-element restrictions are applied without expanding their conditional restitution treatment.

For the commerce and state-order holdings in Parker, Third Circuit No. 95-2018, December 1, 1997, see Constitutional Structure — Interstate support obligations and Federal Courts — Domestic-relations boundaries.

### Statutory offense elements and culpability

#### Bates v. United States

**Docket or dockets:** No. 96-7185; Seventh Circuit No. 95-2203.
**Decided:** November 4, 1997.

##### Whether §1097(a) misapplication requires an additional intent to injure or defraud

**Holding:** For the version of 20 U.S.C. §1097(a) governing the alleged 1990 conduct, knowing and willful culpable misapplication is required, but a separate purpose to injure or defraud is not. An unpaid refund, accounting error, insolvency or funding-condition breach alone does not establish that culpable misapplication.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Ginsburg), unanimous.

**Material application:** The indictment's alleged knowing diversion permits rejection of the additional-purpose requirement at the pretrial stage; it proves no allegation. The distinct revision history of the bank-misapplication statute does not transfer its earlier intent language to §1097, and text, context and statutory history leave no residual ambiguity warranting that extra element.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The complete meaning of willfulness, including knowledge of illegality, and every conversion theory remain open. Statutory silence does not categorically defeat inherited criminal meaning. The later refund amendment is not applied to earlier conduct. Aiding-and-abetting and independent indictment questions are not expanded or presumed preserved.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirmance sustains reinstatement of the indictment on the presented issue, subject to proof of all actual elements and properly available objections; it affirms no conviction or guilt finding and decides no instruction, sentence or evidentiary sufficiency issue.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Wells distinguishes enacted elements from proposed additions without eliminating fault. Ratzlaf preserves contextual willfulness and residual lenity. Staples, Morissette and Liparota retain the culpability required by each particular offense; none supplies a universal mental-state rule or the additional fraudulent purpose requested here.

### False statements and investigative denials

#### Brogan v. United States

**Docket or dockets:** No. 96-1579.

**Decided:** 1998-01-26.

##### A false negative answer has no categorical exemption

**Holding:** The offense-date §1001 does not categorically exclude an otherwise covered, knowingly and willfully false material denial merely because it is a bare response to investigative questioning about the speaker's wrongdoing. Every actual statutory predicate remains necessary; this decision adopts no universal materiality rule for every §1001 clause and decides no immaterial-statement application.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas's Parts I–II, III-A and IV are joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Souter, providing six direct votes for the principal statutory framework. Ginsburg independently agrees with the narrow no-exception proposition in her judgment concurrence. Part III-B(1)'s limited response to the abuse argument has Thomas, Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia and Kennedy; III-B(2)'s further response has only four votes and is not a holding.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Prior investigative knowledge creates no categorical exception, but no distinct materiality issue is decided. The concealment clause’s trick/scheme/device requirement does not govern every false-statement charge. Internal charging policy and unenacted proposals create no defense; potential abuse supplies no categorical exemption, without approving actual abuse.

##### The privilege does not authorize a deliberately false answer

**Holding:** The privilege against compelled self-incrimination provides no general right to answer falsely instead of invoking lawful protection against answering. It therefore supplies no constitutional-avoidance basis for the categorical exculpatory-no exception on this record; actual coercion, a valid privilege claim and unlawful demands for waiver remain distinct questions.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas's principal analysis outside III-B has Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Souter, providing six direct votes. Ginsburg's judgment concurrence is not counted as a join in this entire explanation.

##### Affirmance leaves unpresented element and trial questions unresolved

**Holding:** Rejection of the presented statutory exception leaves the reviewed conviction in force without reopening the separate payment conviction or ordering a proceeding on hypothetical trial errors. The meaning of willfulness concerning knowledge of illegality, actual surprise or lack of reflection, materiality, jury allocation and conduct-time notice remain subject to their own properly presented claims and supported records.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas's opinion: Part IV has the same six direct principal-opinion joins; Ginsburg independently concurs in affirmance and these limits.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No universal definition of willfulness, knowledge-of-illegality rule or warning duty follows. The initial denial preceded the warning; failure to correct is not an additional charged statement. The 1996 amendment is not applied to the 1993 statement.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The reviewed §1001 conviction is affirmed, 7–2, without reopening the employer-payment conviction or ordering trial, sentencing or collateral relief.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Hubbard retains its offense-date institution requirement, its rejection of Bramblett's incompatible all-branch construction and its question-versus-argument boundary. This included executive-agency investigation presents no excluded-court nexus or new litigation exception.
- Gilliland's agency-function rationale is applied without adding successful deception or an independent perversion-of-functions element. It does not exempt simple investigative denials.
- Bryson supplies the privilege boundary against deliberate falsity as a substitute for lawful resistance. Its affidavit and benefit setting is distinguished from the present interview; it does not decide every coercion or oral-statement question.
- Ratzlaf's contextual interpretation and independent residual-lenity principle remain intact. They do not establish automatic ambiguity from circuit disagreement or a universal knowledge-of-illegality requirement.
- Wells, Bates and Salinas retain the actual statutory conduct, institution, transaction and fault predicates of their respective offenses. They inform the distinction between enacted requirements and unsupported additions without transferring their elements to §1001.
- Gaudin's jury-allocation rule and Lanier's conduct-time warning rule remain operative at their stated scopes. Neither establishes a trial error or new remand here.
- LaChance preserves actual employment proof and process while rejecting categorical knowing-denial immunity; it creates no criminal §1001 rule. Garrity and Gardner retain their separate compelled-testimony and waiver protections.

### Civil banking sanctions and successive prosecution

#### Hudson v. United States

**Docket or dockets:** No. 96-976.

**Decided:** 1997-12-10.

##### Civil intent remains rebuttable through examination of actual punitive operation

**Holding:** Congress's civil designation of these banking monetary sanctions and the regulatory character of the occupational restriction establish the intended civil framework. That designation may be overcome by the clearest proof that the sanctions' purpose or effect is criminal; their supported design and operation must be assessed together, without treating deterrence, severity, occupational burden or overlap with crime as individually conclusive.

**Proposition-level authority:** Breyer's Part I, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg; seven Justices.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The original monetary authorization governs; later increased penalty tiers do not apply. Good-faith and gravity considerations affect amount without establishing a universal willfulness element.

##### These monetary assessments survive Halper's retained personal-penalty inquiry

**Independent alternative holding:** A personal civil penalty remains subject to Halper's requirement of a rational remedial relationship, including reasonable approximation; that rule is not overruled or replaced by a universal statutory-face inquiry. On the supported record, the actual $44,000 in total monetary assessments, compared with the District Court's finding of $72,000 in government costs pursuing these defendants, permits a remedial characterization and requires no further accounting.

**Proposition-level authority for alternative holding:** Breyer's Part II, joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg; six Justices. This independent accounting ground does not receive Stone-Zsela's join.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Reasonable approximation of legitimate pursuit costs suffices; the agency need not have calculated costs initially. The $200,000 initial demand is not the $44,000 obligation imposed. No automatic monetary ratio, immunity for every penalty below asserted costs or universal compensation ceiling is established.

##### The combined sanctions do not bar this prosecution

**Holding:** The banking restriction, which permits participation with written regulatory authorization, protects positions of trust in a regulated industry. Considered with the actual financial assessments, its supported operation does not provide the clearest proof of criminal punishment; the subsequent prosecution therefore is not barred on the presented multiple-punishment theory.

**Proposition-level authority:** Breyer's Part III, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg; seven Justices.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The banking restriction permits participation with written regulatory authorization. Different schemes or materially different actual operation may classify differently. No offense-identity, attachment, Excessive Fines or independent due-process question is decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The unanimous judgment affirms reinstatement of the criminal prosecution without an additional accounting hearing or any determination of guilt or offense identity.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

Halper remains binding in its exceptional personal-civil-penalty field and is applied to the supported monetary obligations and costs. Ursery's rebuttable civil classification and preservation of that field remain intact. Hendricks supplies cumulative actual-operation analysis without transferring its confinement outcome. Ward and Mendoza-Martinez retain their intent/effect and punitive-indicator roles. Kurth Ranch's extraordinary-tax holding is distinguished, not narrowed. Dixon, Grady, Blockburger and Felix retain their offense-identity rules; none is applied to determine the identity of these charges. Austin's distinct Excessive Fines protection remains available in an appropriate case. No precedent is overruled.

### Assimilative crimes and federal sentencing

#### Lewis v. United States

**Docket or dockets:** No. 96-7151.

**Decided:** 1998-03-09.

##### The Assimilative Crimes Act fills statutory gaps without rewriting federal offenses

**Holding:** A court applying §13(a) first asks whether a federal enactment punishes the act or omission; ordinarily, an absence of federal punishment permits assimilation. If federal punishment exists, the court must determine whether the federal scheme precludes the particular state provision because, for example, assimilation would interfere with federal policy, rewrite a federally defined offense, or enter a field Congress intended federal law to occupy.

**Proposition-level authority:** Breyer's Part I, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter and Ginsburg: six Justices directly adopt the rule, its qualifications and its application below.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Different labels, wording, jurisdictional terms, elements or penalties alone neither establish nor defeat a gap. Genuinely different wrongs or protected interests may reveal one only when assimilation supplements rather than displaces the federal scheme. No mechanical same-elements, same-conduct or generic-offense rule controls.

##### Federal murder grading leaves no gap for this state murder grade

**Holding:** Louisiana's first-degree-murder provision based on the victim's age is not assimilated for this killing. Section 1111's interwoven first-degree categories and residual second-degree category already comprehensively grade the murder and prescribe its available punishment; adding the State's grade would supplant that scheme.

**Proposition-level authority:** Breyer's Part I and the same six-Justice coalition. Scalia and Thomas also support nonassimilation but do not join this framework.

**Material application:** Section 1111’s first-degree categories and residual second-degree category deliberately cover and grade the killing. Adding Louisiana’s child-age grade and mandatory punishment would replace that choice.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Assimilation of a genuinely different state offense under another federal enactment remains open.

##### The accepted federal conviction requires federal sentencing

**Holding:** The uncontested federal second-degree murder conviction remains intact on the jury's necessary findings, but the sentence must be determined under §1111 and the applicable Sentencing Guidelines. The mere fact that life is within the federal statutory maximum does not establish that the sentence was lawfully selected under that federal scheme; only the sentence affirmance is vacated and remanded.

**Proposition-level authority:** Breyer's Part II, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg: eight Justices.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The accepted federal second-degree conviction remains intact. Only the sentence affirmance is vacated; federal resentencing must apply §1111 and the Guidelines, with any lawful upward departure decided initially by the sentencing court. Life remains available if lawfully justified.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The uncontested necessary jury findings establish no general power to reform convictions over disputed elements, notice, preservation or jury findings. No acquittal or retrial is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Williams v. United States, 327 U.S. 711 (1946), is applied: the ACA supplements federal criminal law but cannot enlarge a federally defined offense or change Congress's selected punishment through a different state definition. Its approach supports the statutory comparison, without a mechanical different-elements permission.
- United States v. Wells, decided February 26, 1997, is preserved and applied at its actual scope: enacted elements govern, and independent conviction and sentencing questions remain distinct. It does not itself resolve ACA displacement.
- United States v. Gaudin, decided June 19, 1995, is preserved: juries decide actual criminal elements. It does not create an element or an unraised defect in the accepted jury findings here.

### Federal bribery and RICO conspiracy elements

#### Salinas v. United States

**Docket or dockets:** No. 96-738.
**Decided:** December 2, 1997.

##### Whether §666 requires a demonstrable effect on federal funds

**Holding:** Section 666 does not add proof of a demonstrable effect on federal funds to its actual institutional, transactional and culpability requirements. The Government must still prove agency, corrupt acceptance of a thing of value to influence or reward, covered entity business or transactions valued at least $5,000, and qualifying federal benefits exceeding $10,000 in the relevant year; §666(c)'s exception for bona fide compensation and ordinary-course expenses remains effective.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Thomas), unanimous.

**Material application:** The corrupt contact-visit arrangement in the federally connected jail permits rejection of traced federal dollars as an additional element. The $5,000 threshold concerns the business, transaction or series, not necessarily the value of the bribe, watches or truck.

**Limits and questions not reached:** A distinct statutory requirement connecting the bribe to expenditure of federal funds and the statute's outer constitutional reach are separately reserved; neither reservation establishes a nexus test or burden. Qualifying-benefits status is assumed here, not established for every federal receipt. The decision determines no precise annual amount or intangible-benefit coverage and does not reopen valuation.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The two bribery convictions are affirmed on this statutory question without independent sentencing review or a new remedial remand.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Wells distinguishes genuine elements from additions without itself construing §666. Ratzlaf preserves contextual interpretation and residual lenity; neither entails the proposed demonstrable-effects condition.

##### Whether a RICO conspirator must personally commit or promise two predicate acts

**Holding:** Section 1962(d) requires knowing agreement to advance an undertaking that would satisfy the substantive enterprise offense if carried out, without requiring each conspirator personally to commit or agree to commit two predicates. The agreement must concern an unlawful object encompassing the substantive elements, including Reves's direction requirement; association, awareness, friendship or employment alone is insufficient.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Thomas), unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Reves's substantive requirement does not automatically require personal direction of the enterprise by every conspirator. Rejecting the personal-two-act instruction objection approves neither the entire charge nor an independent personal-direction instruction. General sufficiency, harmlessness, aiding-and-abetting liability and a general overt-act rule are not decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The conspiracy conviction is affirmed on the presented question, leaving the distinct §1962(c) acquittal undisturbed and ordering no new trial or special remand.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Shabani supports the distinction between agreement and personal performance within its own statutory scope. Reves remains the substantive enterprise rule and had left conspiracy open; its substantive object is preserved without transferring every performance requirement to each conspirator. Wells and Ratzlaf preserve actual statutory conditions, contextual meaning and residual lenity.

### Habeas procedural default and appellate initiative

#### Trest v. Cain

**Docket or dockets:** No. 96-7901; review of Trest v. Whitley, Fifth Circuit No. 94-40515.
**Decided:** December 9, 1997.

##### Whether an appellate court must invoke an omitted state procedural-default defense

**Holding:** An appellate court is not obliged to raise state procedural default that the State has not asserted; default does not deprive the federal court of subject-matter jurisdiction. This absence of duty decides neither whether a court has discretion to raise the defense nor when such action would be proper, including in this case.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Stevens), unanimous.

**Material application:** The Fifth Circuit treated predicted Mississippi untimeliness as requiring exclusion and relied on Sones without party discussion. Affirmance on a discretionary ground the court did not give would replace its actual rationale and assume undeveloped predicates. Comity and federalism do not supply an independent command to invoke the defense.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Omission establishes neither intentional waiver nor automatic forfeiture. Actual adequate independent default, cause and prejudice, miscarriage of justice, plea validity and a route to challenge expired predicate convictions remain distinct and unresolved. Under Lindh, the covered chapter 153 amendments do not govern this noncapital application filed September 30, 1991; amended §2254(b)(3)'s exhaustion-waiver rule supplies no default rule here, while separately directed provisions retain their scope. Any lawful later consideration requires attention to the actual ground and an appropriate response opportunity, without a universal constitutional additional-briefing rule for every unargued issue.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Fifth Circuit judgment is vacated for further proceedings free of the supposed mandatory duty. The Court grants no writ, invalidates no conviction, reduces no sentence and requires no automatic evidentiary hearing or predetermined default outcome.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Lambrix establishes default's nonjurisdictional character and rejects inflexible sequencing; its preserved-defense remand does not require revival of an omitted defense. Granberry's treatment of omitted exhaustion supplies an analogy against compulsion, not a resolution of discretionary default initiative. Coleman retains its adequate-independent-ground predicates, supported anticipated-default inquiry and cause/prejudice or miscarriage-of-justice exceptions; its stay action establishes neither jurisdictional classification nor a new default. Schlup's innocence gateway remains distinct. Lindh supplies the temporal limit. Gray creates no general automatic forfeiture rule for omitted default defenses.

### Retrospective punishment and civil notification

#### W.P. v. Verniero

**Docket or dockets:** Third Circuit No. 96-5416; the distinct E.B. appeal, No. 96-5132, is outside review.

**Decided:** 1998-03-16.

##### The challenged notification is not punishment on this record

**Holding:** For the class whose offenses preceded October 31, 1994 and who face Tier 2 or Tier 3 notification, New Jersey's civil public-protection purpose remains unrebutted by the clearest proof of punitive purpose or effect in the scheme's supported combined operation. The challenged notification therefore supplies no Ex Post Facto or Double Jeopardy ground for relief; the Court decides no same-offense question.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of Souter's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Ginsburg and Breyer; six Justices directly adopt the common civil-classification ground. Scalia and Thomas support rejection on their separate grounds; Stevens dissents on Ex Post Facto punishment and does not reach independent Double Jeopardy identity.

**Material application:** Risk grading, individual exceptions and audience tailoring link disclosure to public protection. Tier 2 reaches qualifying schools, child-care facilities and organizations serving children or supporting women, under the likely-encounter restriction; Tier 3 reaches persons likely to encounter the registrant. The scheme imposes no direct residence, movement, employment or licensing prohibition.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Severe disclosure and private consequences are not categorically irrelevant, but do not establish the demanded punitive proof on this record. Registration alone, Tier 1, post-enactment offenses, materially different operation and every later registry are undecided. Same-offense identity is not reached.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Ursery, Ward and Mendoza-Martinez supply rebuttable civil classification and combined purpose/effect examination; neither labels nor a single indicator is conclusive.
- Hendricks retains its as-applied punitive-confinement holding and actual-operation method; its outcome is distinguished, not replaced with an approval of that custody.
- Hudson preserves aggregate examination and Halper in its separate personal-monetary field. Halper is not extended into a notification accounting test.
- Kurth Ranch retains its extraordinary-tax punishment holding; its arrest-linked operation differs from the tailored warnings here.
- Blockburger and retained Grady offense-identity questions are not reached. No precedent is overruled.

For this case’s other questions, see Civil Rights (The State must justify both tier and notification plan before ordinary notification; Risk classification requires clear and convincing proof; the plan injunction is retained on a narrower authority basis); Federal Courts (The advance-notice emergency question remains unripe).

### Aggravated reentry and the charged offense

#### Almendarez-Torres v. United States

**Docket or dockets:** No. 96-6839.

**Decided:** 1998-03-24.

##### Section 1326(b)(2) requires an aggravated-offense allegation before its higher maximum may apply

**Holding:** The prior aggravated-felony conviction specified in §1326(b)(2) is an element of the aggravated unlawful-reentry offense. It must be alleged in the federal indictment and, unless validly admitted as an element of the charged offense, proved to the jury beyond a reasonable doubt; an admission to the fact of a prior conviction does not cure its omission from this indictment, so the existing conviction permits no sentence above §1326(a)'s two-year maximum.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of Scalia's opinion is joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg. These five expressly adopt the statutory rule, its ordinary element consequences and the remedy. Four explain the construction through constitutional avoidance; Stone-Zsela gives a definitive constitutional ground separately. No majority adopts a general constitutional rule governing all facts that increase a statutory maximum.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The rule is statutory. No majority decides a general constitutional rule for maximum-increasing facts or a constitutional prior-conviction exception. The admitted convictions remain valid; neither subsidiary proof questions nor facts that leave the maximum unchanged are decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The enhanced sentence is vacated and the judgment reversed and remanded for sentencing within the two-year maximum on this indictment and plea. The plea is not withdrawn and no acquittal follows. The availability of a properly charged further proceeding is reserved.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **Hamling v. United States:** Applied to require an indictment alleging each element of the aggravated offense.
- **United States v. Gaudin:** Applied after statutory classification; its jury-and-proof rule for actual elements remains controlling without deciding all sentencing classifications.
- **McMillan v. Pennsylvania:** Distinguished because its mandatory minimum left the statutory maximum unchanged; its limited holding remains intact.
- **United States v. Watts / United States v. Putra:** Preserved for ordinary sentencing within punishment authorized by the conviction; it does not resolve this higher-maximum classification.
- **Custis v. United States:** Distinguished as a collateral-review decision; the existing limits on attacking prior convictions remain intact.
- **Old Chief v. United States:** Applied to the distinction between element status and the form of adequate prior-conviction proof; no broader compulsory-stipulation rule is created.

### Reliability restrictions on defense evidence

#### United States v. Scheffer

**Docket or dockets:** No. 96-1133.

**Decided:** 1998-03-31.

##### Supported reliability concerns justify this categorical polygraph rule against the presented constitutional challenge

**Holding:** The constitutional right to present a defense permits evidentiary restrictions justified by legitimate trial interests and proportionate to their purposes.
