This holding concerns timely objection, a definitive ruling and defensive disclosure. Silence, tentative rulings, failure to testify, independent affirmative use, express consent, waiver and invited error remain distinct; broader constitutional questions are not decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Ninth Circuit's preservation bar is reversed. Admissibility and any resulting harmless-error question return for ordinary appellate decision. The trial convictions are not vacated, and the Court orders neither acquittal nor an automatic new trial.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **Luce v. United States, 469 U.S. 38:** Distinguished because Ohler testified; its no-testimony preservation holding remains intact.
- **United States v. Olano:** Applied to distinguish an actual surrender of the right from conduct that merely mitigates an adverse ruling; its forfeited-error framework is unchanged.
- **New York v. Hill:** Applied at the actual-consent boundary; it creates no general presumption that a defensive tactic waives an objection.
- **United States v. Martinez-Salazar:** Applied for the distinction between responding to an error and surrendering the underlying objection; it is not extended into a universal compelled-choice rule.
- **Old Chief v. United States:** Its distinction between contextual evidence choices and independent whole-record harmlessness supports the preservation rule; its status-only adequate-substitute holding is not expanded to govern every impeachment use.

### Authentication of electronic conversations

#### United States v. Tank

**Docket or dockets:** No separate Supreme Court docket supplied; judgment below 200 F.3d 627.
**Decided:** February 21, 2001.

##### Whether participant testimony and identifying circumstances authenticate partial electronic conversations

**Holding:** Knowledgeable-participant testimony explaining recording and the accuracy of retained conversations, combined with circumstances sufficiently connecting a screen name to the accused, can satisfy Rule 901 even when unrelated material was deleted and electronic alteration was possible. The combined foundation here suffices; the judge must still exclude or require proper further foundation when an identified defect leaves no reasonable basis to find the offered record is what its proponent claims.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Souter), unanimous.

**Material application:** The claimed exhibit consists of retained partial conversations. Firsthand recording and accuracy testimony, disclosure of deletions, the limited screen-name admission and participant identifications supply a reasonable foundation together. Neither every line nor an unedited archive must be independently verified. Rule 104(b) leaves ultimate authenticity to the jury; Rule 104(a) does not require a conclusive judicial authenticity finding.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The trial court must assess concrete defects; possible electronic alteration and disclosed deletion alone do not defeat this foundation. No mandatory forensic certificate or expert checklist applies to firsthand participant testimony. Foundation application receives abuse-of-discretion review with correction of legal error. Authentication establishes neither completeness, truth, constitutional reliability nor guilt; independent hearsay, completeness and prejudice rules remain. The search, general sufficiency, confrontation and sentencing issues are outside review, and no harmlessness holding is reached.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The convictions are affirmed against the authentication challenge. The existing Ninth Circuit resentencing remand for grouping, role adjustment and the inconsistent calculation remains operative; the original 235-month sentence is not reinstated. No exclusion, acquittal or retrial is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Huddleston v. United States: applies Rule 104(b)'s sufficient-evidence allocation to the conditional authenticity question; the jury retains ultimate assessment.
- United States v. Hale: preserves the distinction between authentication and other evidentiary or constitutional functions; its accepted public-record certifications do not govern the identity of private logs.
- Daubert, General Electric v. Joiner and Kumho Tire: preserve proffer-specific expert screening and legal-error correction; no expert checklist or Rule 104(a) preponderance test is transferred to this participant authentication.
- Old Chief: preserves distinct Rule 403 and whole-record harmlessness inquiries. Because admission is upheld, the Court reaches no harmlessness question and changes no burden or remedial rule.

### Civil commitment and retrospective punishment

#### Seling v. Young, 531 U.S. 250

**Docket or dockets:** No. 99-1185
**Decided:** January 17, 2001.

##### Whether a civil commitment label forecloses proof of punitive operation

**Holding:** A court first identifies whether the legislature intended a civil or criminal commitment scheme; civil intent may be overcome only by the clearest proof that the challenged commitment's purpose or effect is punitive. Actual design and operation must be assessed cumulatively, including contrary civil evidence: a civil label is not conclusive, but confinement, connection to prior crime, indeterminate duration, criminal-style safeguards, deficient treatment or an individual treatment failure alone establishes neither punishment nor a right to release.

**Proposition-level authority:** Breyer's unanimous opinion of the Court.

**Material application:** The court must consider combined reliable direct and circumstantial proof alongside genuine civil evidence. Young's allegations and the separate Turay injunction or reported progress are not conclusive findings of punishment, compliance or cure. Statutory review and release procedures matter without conclusively proving how custody actually operates.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No single conditions defect, treatment failure, confinement feature or misconduct establishes punishment. The Court requires neither a statistical study nor successful cure, and makes no present punishment finding.

##### The available punishment remand and the distinct predicates for custody relief

**Holding:** The Ninth Circuit's reviewed punishment-remand portion is affirmed, preserving the opportunity to establish Young's supported claim under the former chapter 153 habeas framework applicable to his 1994 application. If the clearest proof establishes punishment and the challenged custody retroactively adds it for conduct predating enactment, custody under that unlawful application must end through appropriate habeas relief, while independent lawful custody is preserved; a conditions violation that leaves custody lawfully civil instead requires relief through its proper conditions channel.

**Proposition-level authority:** Breyer's unanimous opinion of the Court.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Lindh places the 1994 application under former chapter 153 rather than amended section 2254(d); separately directed provisions and ordinary habeas requirements still apply. Punishment and retroactivity require separate findings. Double-jeopardy relief additionally requires the pertinent prior jeopardy and a second punishment for the same offense if that claim remains necessary; no such independent entitlement is established.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the reviewed punishment-remand portion with instructions. The existing opportunity to prove punitive operation remains, but no universal hearing right, present writ, release or replacement custody authority is supplied. The unpetitioned due-process, equal-protection and commitment-procedure rulings remain undisturbed.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Kansas v. Hendricks: applies its rebuttable civil-intent and clearest-proof framework, cumulative operational inquiry and retrospective-punishment consequence; its particular Kansas facts are not transferred to Young.
- Hudson v. United States: applies its preserved design-and-operation inquiry; it did not replace actual-operation review with a statutory-face-only rule or overrule Halper in its personal-monetary-penalty field.
- United States v. Ursery: preserves the demanding rebuttal of civil classification without transferring its forfeiture outcome to personal confinement.
- United States v. Ward and Kennedy v. Mendoza-Martinez: preserve the intent-and-effect inquiry and cumulative use of punitive indicators, without a conclusive single factor or new exhaustive checklist.
- Lindh v. Murphy: applies the pre-enactment application rule, preserving separately directed provisions rather than exempting every habeas issue from every amendment.

### Federal fraud offenses and property

#### Cleveland v. United States, 531 U.S. 12

**Docket or dockets:** No. 99-804.
**Decided:** November 7, 2000.

##### Whether sovereign licensing permission is property in the State's hands

**Holding:** Authority held solely as sovereign regulator to issue, withhold, renew, regulate, or revoke a license is not State property under 18 U.S.C. section 1341. Louisiana's video-poker permission has that regulatory character; economic value to its recipient does not supply the charged State-property element, whether deception obtains initial issuance or renewal.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas's unanimous Opinion of the Court, including the renewal application.

**Material application:** The State transfers regulatory permission, rather than an owned commercial asset. Licensing revenue expectations and continuing power to exclude, limit transfers, or revoke do not change the object charged in the two 1994 and 1995 renewal counts into money or proprietary assets.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The ruling does not deny a license's value to its holder or classify every governmental franchise. Schemes directed at actual public money, unpaid revenue, or commercial property require their own analysis. Materiality was outside the grant, and no independent honest-services theory was presented.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The two licensing-based mail-fraud convictions are set aside. On remand, the actual indictment, verdict, and judgment determine which other convictions and sentences depend on those predicates. Independent convictions and surviving independent bases remain intact; no separate tax, laundering, racketeering, or unreviewed-theory acquittal is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** McNally v. United States preserves the money-or-property boundary. Carpenter v. United States protects genuine commercial intangible property without converting regulatory permission into ownership. Neder v. United States, June 10, 1999, retains materiality as a separate element and does not replace the missing property object.

##### Whether the acquisition clause independently reaches value created in the recipient's hands

**Holding:** Section 1341's false-pretenses acquisition clause describes a method of property fraud and retains the requirement that the charged scheme target property in the deceived victim's hands. Value acquired by the recipient alone cannot support the alternative license-acquisition theory.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas's unanimous opinion directly answers this separate statutory contention.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The victim-property rule concerns the scheme's object; it does not require completed economic loss or actual reliance. It supplies no alternative ground to sustain either renewal conviction.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** McNally governs the statute as a whole, including the acquisition clause. Neder's separate rejection of completed-loss and actual-reliance requirements remains unchanged.

### Habeas limitations and state collateral review

#### Artuz v. Bennett, 531 U.S. 4

**Docket or dockets:** No. 99-1238.
**Decided:** November 7, 2000.

##### Proper filing of a collateral application containing claims subject to mandatory relief bars

**Holding:** Section 2244(d)(2) treats a state collateral application as properly filed when its delivery and acceptance satisfy the requirements for filing that application. A claim-specific rule requiring denial of relief does not defeat proper filing; New York Criminal Procedure Law sections 440.10(2)(a) and (c) are relief rules of that kind.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's unanimous Opinion of the Court.

**Material application:** Paragraph (a) requires denial of an issue previously resolved on its merits on appeal unless controlling law subsequently changes with retroactive effect. Paragraph (c) requires an adequate underlying record for appellate review and an unjustifiable failure to take or perfect a timely appeal or raise the issue on a perfected appeal. These inquiries determine entitlement to claim relief, not delivery or acceptance of the application. No finding applies either bar or its qualifications to a particular Bennett claim.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Clerical acceptance does not excuse an actual filing defect. Form, delivery deadlines, proper court or office, fees, and genuine filing preconditions remain distinct; the Court does not classify every rule without its terms. Whether exceptions to a filing deadline can preserve proper filing of a late application remains undecided. The absence of a written state denial is Bennett's allegation and the lower court's assumption, not this Court's finding. Actual pendency, transitional grace periods, exhaustion, procedural default, and habeas entitlement remain unresolved.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Second Circuit is affirmed and its remand remains operative. Timeliness must be calculated without treating the two relief bars as filing defects, with open dates and defenses adjudicated in further proceedings. No writ, retrial, or release is awarded.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Lindh v. Murphy, June 23, 1997, retains its provision-specific temporal construction; it neither exempts Bennett's post-enactment federal application nor answers proper filing.

### Ineffective assistance at sentencing

#### Glover v. United States, 531 U.S. 198

**Docket or dockets:** No. 99-8576
**Decided:** January 9, 2001.

##### Whether sentencing prejudice requires a minimum duration or offense-level increase

**Holding:** When deficient representation allegedly causes a longer prison sentence, Strickland prejudice requires a reasonable probability of a shorter lawful sentence, sufficient to undermine confidence in the result; it contains no additional minimum measured in months of confinement or in offense levels. An alleged six-to-twenty-one-month increase caused by a two-level sentencing error therefore cannot be rejected as constitutionally insignificant, but deficient performance, the underlying error and the required causal probability must still be established.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's unanimous opinion of the Court.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The two granted questions assume deficient representation and calculation error. Neither a changed range nor counsel's mistake alone proves prejudice. Correct grouping, counsel's performance, the complete lawful calculation, and available preserved independent defenses remain unresolved.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the denial of section 2255 relief on the categorical prejudice ground and remand for the remaining claim. No sentence reduction or release is directed.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Strickland v. Washington, 466 U.S. 668 (1984): applies its separate performance and reasonable-probability requirements to an alleged increase in imprisonment; neither requirement acquires a minimum-duration or minimum-offense-level condition.
- Lockhart v. Fretwell, 506 U.S. 364 (1993): applies its distinction between an unavailable substantive benefit and a lawful sentencing protection; its limited unavailable-benefit rule remains intact and does not support a significance threshold.
- Williams v. Taylor (Terry Williams), 529 U.S. 362 (2000): applies its ordinary lawful-result prejudice rule. Its state-prisoner statutory review and actual capital-sentencing findings do not decide this federal prisoner's unresolved performance and calculation issues.

### Proof of the statutory offense

#### Fiore v. White, 531 U.S. 225

**Docket or dockets:** No. 98-942
**Decided:** January 9, 2001.

##### Conviction without proof of an element that state law always required

**Holding:** A State may not sustain a conviction when its authoritative account of the law in force at the relevant time identifies a necessary element and the State concedes it produced no evidence of that element. Pennsylvania's clarification that actual absence of the required permit was always an element of the owner/operator offense therefore establishes a due-process violation in both of Fiore's challenged no-permit convictions; deviation from a permit's terms cannot substitute for its absence.

**Proposition-level authority:** Unanimous per curiam opinion.

**Material application:** The authoritative Pennsylvania answer establishes that absence of the permit was an element when both convictions became final. The Commonwealth concedes possession of the permit; deviation from its conditions cannot prove its absence.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The holding concerns both owner/operator no-permit convictions, one from each trial, and leaves the other convictions intact. It decides no retroactivity rule for genuinely new state constructions and substitutes no separate environmental offense.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse and remand. Relief against both convictions and their attributable custody depends on actual preserved habeas prerequisites; independent lawful custody remains. For those prerequisites, see Fiore v. White, January 9, 2001, No. 98-942, Federal Courts - Habeas relief after a missing-element determination.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- In re Winship: applies its proof-beyond-a-reasonable-doubt requirement to an existing state-law element; the seriousness of other conduct cannot supply the omitted proof.
- Jackson v. Virginia: applies its protection against conviction without evidence sufficient to establish each necessary element.
- Fiore v. White, November 30, 1999: completes the reserved federal inquiry after the authoritative state answer; the earlier certification itself supplied neither state-law meaning nor a federal merits judgment.

### Involuntary pretrial medication and advance defense protection

#### United States v. Weston

**Docket or dockets:** D.C. Circuit No. 99-3119; judgment below, 206 F.3d 9.
**Decided:** April 17, 2001.

##### Adequate justification for unwanted antipsychotic medication of a pretrial defendant

**Holding:** Over an objecting pretrial defendant's refusal, the Government must establish medical appropriateness and overriding need while considering reasonable less intrusive means. The present safety justification fails because the evidence establishes containment of immediate danger through isolation and continuous observation and the order does not adequately assess that alternative.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Part I, unanimously joined; this ground independently sustains affirmance.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court does not require indefinite isolation or find it harmless or permanently preferable. Staff-contact risk and the medical and practical costs of isolation remain relevant; changed circumstances or a sufficient independent safety showing can support a future order.

**Independent alternative holding:** When restoration of competency is an actual medication purpose and no independently sufficient safety justification is established, the judge must evaluate reasonably foreseeable effects on comprehension, consultation, testimony, demeanor and defense presentation before authorizing treatment. Available clinical evidence, reasonable alternatives and workable safeguards must support that assessment; exact prediction is unnecessary, review after treatment alone is inadequate, and actual effects must be reassessed before trial.

**Proposition-level authority for alternative holding:** O'Connor's Part II, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer: seven Justices. Scalia and Thomas do not join it.

**Limits and questions not reached:** This preliminary inquiry is not a complete sufficient restoration standard. An insanity defense does not invariably entitle a defendant to display untreated symptoms; recordings, explanation and dose adjustment must be assessed rather than presumed adequate. No majority rule covers a materially different order with an independently sufficient safety basis. Prosecution alone as an ultimate justification, emergency treatment, execution treatment, capital medical ethics, discharge, dismissal and guilt remain undecided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm and remand, leaving the medication authorization set aside. Any renewed order must meet the liberty requirements and, where Part II applies, advance and pretrial effects review. No release, competency determination, fixed regimen or prohibition of voluntary treatment follows.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Riggins supplies the refusal-interest protection and is extended to the stated advance defense-effects inquiry; its complete ultimate-restoration question remains open. Harper retains justified confinement-safety treatment and is not a categorical answer to pretrial restoration or defense protection.

### Fair warning and retrospective judicial removal of an offense limit

#### Rogers v. Tennessee, 532 U.S. 451

**Docket or dockets:** No. 99-6218.
**Decided:** May 14, 2001.

##### Whether abolition of the acknowledged year-and-a-day limit may support this earlier murder charge

**Holding:** Tennessee's acknowledged year-and-a-day limitation remained operative when Rogers acted; its later abolition cannot support his murder conviction on this record. Medical obsolescence, infrequent litigation and abandonment elsewhere justify prospective reform but do not adequately warn of retrospective removal of the conceded offense boundary.

**Proposition-level authority:** Breyer's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia and Thomas; five direct joins to this application and remedy, without universal equivalence between judicial and legislative change.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Subjective reliance and innocence of the assault are unnecessary. Medical proof of causation does not eliminate the legal interval. Later authorities cannot supply conduct-time notice; ordinary interpretation, foreseeable clarification and prospective reform remain possible.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse and remand for vacation of the murder conviction resting on retrospective abolition and determination of an independently lawful remaining disposition. The undisputed interval is not reopened for proof of earlier death. No particular lesser conviction, sentence, retrial or release independent of other lawful custody is directed.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Bouie and Lanier supply objective warning from the law before the conduct, not wrongfulness alone. Carmell retains its legislative proof-sufficiency rule without directly applying the Ex Post Facto Clause to courts. Fiore's clarification of an existing element is distinguished from acknowledged abolition.

##### What general due-process framework governs ordinary common-law development

**Holding:** Ordinary common-law development is permissible, but due process forbids retrospective enlargement that is unexpected and indefensible in light of previously expressed law. Judicial development does not automatically inherit every legislative category of the Ex Post Facto Clause.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's dissent, joined by Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg, and Breyer's express concurrence adoption supply five direct agreements to this framework despite their different application results. This is an actual majority proposition across writings, not a Marks synthesis or an inference from the judgment coalition.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Bouie and Lanier preserve objective fair warning with room for foreseeable interpretation. Calder and Collins retain their legislative categories without automatic transposition to every judicial clarification. The stronger structural concurrence does not become a Court rule.

### Medical necessity and prohibited marijuana distribution

#### United States v. Oakland Cannabis Buyers' Cooperative, 532 U.S. 483

**Docket or dockets:** No. 00-151.
**Decided:** May 14, 2001.

##### Whether the Cooperative has a medical-necessity defense under the Controlled Substances Act

**Holding:** The Act provides no medical-necessity exception for the Cooperative's prohibited marijuana manufacture, distribution or possession with intent to distribute. Neither medical recommendation nor state permission supplies federal authorization, even if the recipient meets the lower court's medical criteria.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's opinion of the Court, unanimous among eight participants; Breyer takes no part.

**Material application:** The lower exception required all four conditions: serious medical illness; imminent harm without cannabis; a need for cannabis to treat or alleviate the condition or associated symptoms; and no reasonable lawful effective alternative because alternatives tried either failed or produced intolerable side effects. The Court assumes no finding that every recipient met those conditions; even satisfaction cannot authorize this distributor, which claims no research authorization.

**Limits and questions not reached:** This statutory holding is no finding that cannabis cannot medically benefit a patient. Individual possession, individual-patient necessity and injunctions, and necessity under materially different statutes remain outside the case. Constitutional avoidance supplies no textual exception, but preserved Commerce Clause, substantive-due-process and patient-liberty objections remain unadjudicated on their own requirements.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse and remand, 8-0, rejecting the medical-distribution basis for injunction modification and the later exception resting on that basis. No criminal punishment, revived moot contempt, decision of the separate government appeal or finding about an individual patient follows.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Rutherford respects the enacted medical-regulatory scheme; Bailey's necessity background and lawful-alternative limits do not override this statute's distribution policy or decide necessity under every federal statute.

For the distinct limits and surviving discretion under section 882(a), see Federal Courts, this case, May 14, 2001, No. 00-151.

### Interstate detainer return before trial

#### Alabama v. Bozeman, 533 U.S. 146

**Docket or dockets:** No. 00-492.
**Decided:** June 11, 2001.

##### Return before trial requires the receiving State to dismiss the covered charges

**Holding:** When a receiving State obtains a prisoner under Article IV and returns him to his original place of imprisonment before trial on the covered charges, Article IV(e) requires dismissal of those charges with prejudice absent a legally effective waiver. This holding applies to the receiving-state regime governing this transfer, not the separate federal receiving-jurisdiction modification. A brief stay, arraignment, absence of prejudice, perceived rehabilitative benefit or later timely trial does not itself provide such an exception or waiver.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of Ginsburg's opinion has the direct joins of all nine Justices and controls.

**Independent alternative holding:** Even assuming that a purpose-based triviality exception could be recognized, a receiving State cannot establish it merely by asserting that returning the prisoner to his original institution improves rehabilitation. That asserted benefit would accompany virtually every premature return and does not make this violation trivial.

**Proposition-level authority for alternative holding:** Part II of Ginsburg's opinion, joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Breyer, supplies six direct votes for this independently sufficient alternative. Stone-Zsela, Scalia and Thomas do not join it.

**Material application:** Alabama obtained custody under Article IV, arraigned Bozeman and returned him to federal prison before trial. Article IX's purposes cannot erase Article IV(e)'s mandatory event-and-remedy provision. Even on the assumed triviality theory, return to the sending institution's rehabilitation programs would accompany virtually every premature return and would swallow the rule.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No legally effective antishuttling waiver is established. Hill's authorized, express agreement before the Article III deadline to a specified later trial date, absent timely objection, conflict or lack of authority, does not waive the compact wholesale. The requirements for another prisoner's actual antishuttling waiver and constitutional speedy-trial issues are not decided. The assumed alternative adopts no triviality exception or rehabilitation element. When the United States receives a prisoner, section 9(1) separately permits dismissal with or without prejudice but requires consideration of offense seriousness, the dismissal circumstances and reprosecution's effect on administration of the agreement and justice, among other factors. Section 9(2) separately permits return without violation under an appropriate court's order after reasonable notice to the prisoner and the United States and opportunity for a hearing. Neither federal-receiver provision applies to Alabama as receiver.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Alabama judgment is affirmed and the covered charges must be dismissed with prejudice. Neither a prejudice hearing nor discretionary balancing is authorized. Ministerial implementation cannot create a fresh waiver inquiry or permit reprosecution of those charges; unrelated charges are not adjudicated.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Anderson v. Yungkau: applied for the mandatory force of the enacted command.
- United States v. Montalvo-Murillo: distinguished because the statute there prescribed no express remedy; Article IV(e) does.
- Wisconsin Department of Revenue v. William Wrigley, Jr., Co.: its background triviality principle yields to the compact's contrary express command.
- New York v. Hill: preserved at its actual authorized, express scheduling-agreement scope; it establishes no waiver of antishuttling protection from silence or perceived benefit.
- Reed v. Farley: preserved as a collateral-review threshold and distinguished from direct enforcement of the compact's remedy.

### Forfeited plea-colloquy error and the appellate record

#### United States v. Vonn, 535 U.S. 55

**Docket or dockets:** No. 00-973.

**Decided:** March 4, 2002.

##### I. Forfeited Rule 11 error is governed by Rule 52(b)

**Holding:** A defendant who forfeits a Rule 11 objection must satisfy Rule 52(b): there must be error that is plain and affects substantial rights, ordinarily with the defendant bearing the effect burden, and correction remains discretionary only when the error seriously affects the fairness, integrity or public reputation of judicial proceedings. Rule 11(h) does not exempt this plea-taking omission from ordinary forfeiture review; intentional waiver remains distinct.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of O'Connor's opinion has the direct support of Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Those eight votes control; Stevens dissents from this rule.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The omitted personal warning remains error despite counsel's presence and the prosecutor's intervention. An objection directed to a different plea-withdrawal issue did not preserve this omission. Substantial-rights effect does not alone require correction, and actual innocence is not an additional prerequisite. The case concerns direct review of this forfeited rule violation, not every constitutional plea defect or collateral remedy.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- United States v. Olano: applied to Rule 11 forfeiture with its usual substantial-rights burden and independent discretionary condition; no automatic reversal, innocence requirement or comprehensive resolution of its reserved categories follows.
- Johnson v. United States: applied at its actual forfeiture and discretionary-correction scope; its particular intervening-law application becomes no general guilt-based exception.
- McCarthy v. United States: its mandatory personal-colloquy principle remains, while the later Rule 11(h) text precludes importing its automatic-reversal consequence into the present rules.
- United States v. Young, 470 U.S. 1: applied to relevant whole-record review of the error's effect, not as permission to omit the required warning.
- Boykin v. Alabama: its constitutional plea requirements remain distinct; this decision supplies no general constitutional-invalidity or collateral-review rule.

##### II. Relevant portions of the existing record inform the error's effect

**Holding:** In assessing the effect of the Rule 11 omission on substantial rights, a reviewing court may consider relevant information throughout the existing record, including earlier judicial rights advisements. Such information bears on actual understanding and effect; it neither cures the judge's omitted personal warning nor conclusively establishes understanding from representation or a signed form alone.

**Proposition-level authority:** All nine Justices join Part II of O'Connor's opinion, giving direct unanimous authority independent of the division over burdens.

**Material application:** The magistrate's two earlier counsel-right advisements and acknowledgments may inform understanding, subject to their content, timing and demonstrated comprehension. Representation or a signed form is not a conclusive substitute for the judge's personal duty.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- United States v. Olano: applied to Rule 11 forfeiture with its usual substantial-rights burden and independent discretionary condition; no automatic reversal, innocence requirement or comprehensive resolution of its reserved categories follows.
- Johnson v. United States: applied at its actual forfeiture and discretionary-correction scope; its particular intervening-law application becomes no general guilt-based exception.
- McCarthy v. United States: its mandatory personal-colloquy principle remains, while the later Rule 11(h) text precludes importing its automatic-reversal consequence into the present rules.
- United States v. Young, 470 U.S. 1: applied to relevant whole-record review of the error's effect, not as permission to omit the required warning.
- Boykin v. Alabama: its constitutional plea requirements remain distinct; this decision supplies no general constitutional-invalidity or collateral-review rule.

##### III. The proper framework must first be applied below

**Holding:** The Ninth Circuit's use of the wrong burden and its exclusion of relevant existing-record material require vacatur and remand. That court must apply all four Rule 52(b) conditions, including record-specific effect and the independent basis for discretionary correction, before deciding the appropriate appellate relief.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part III of O'Connor's opinion has the same eight-Justice coalition as Part I. Stevens agrees with vacatur and remand for whole-record consideration but would apply the Government's harmlessness burden.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The perjury-warning contention remains for its proper procedural treatment because it was unresolved below and outside the reviewed questions.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The appellate judgment is vacated unanimously; eight Justices require application of all four Rule 52(b) conditions. The mandate neither conclusively reinstates the convictions nor authorizes plea withdrawal without that inquiry.

### Conflicted counsel and the duty to inquire

#### Mickens v. Taylor, 535 U.S. 162

**Docket or dockets:** No. 00-9285.
**Decided:** March 27, 2002.

##### An omitted inquiry does not itself establish the adverse effect necessary here

**Holding:** Outside the established circumstances requiring automatic relief, a trial court's failure to inquire into a particular potential conflict it knew or reasonably should have known about does not itself establish a Sixth Amendment violation. A defendant invoking the conflict-based relaxation of ordinary outcome prejudice must at least show that competing obligations adversely affected counsel's performance; that showing is different from proving a reasonable probability of a different verdict under Strickland.

**Proposition-level authority:** Justice Kennedy's Opinion of the Court is joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices O'Connor, Scalia and Thomas. Those five directly adopt the rule, the confined application and affirmance. The four dissenters support a new trial on distinct grounds and do not establish a common alternative holding.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Automatic relief under Holloway remains available for compelled conflicted joint representation after an adequate timely objection. Wood supplies no actual-conflict-only exemption from adverse effect here, and Cronic's exceptional loss-of-assistance conditions are not established by the omitted inquiry. The Court assumes Sullivan applies to this successive representation without deciding its application to every successive-representation category. Post-death confidentiality survives, but proves neither a particular confidence nor impaired performance. The separate factual challenge to the federal hearing's no-adverse-effect finding is outside the grant.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Denial of habeas relief is affirmed on the reviewed conflict claim. The conviction and sentence stand; omitted inquiry alone requires neither a new trial nor another conflict hearing.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **Holloway v. Arkansas, 435 U.S. 475:** Preserved for compelled joint representation over an adequate timely objection; its automatic remedy is not extended solely because the judge knew a potential conflict.
- **Cuyler v. Sullivan, 446 U.S. 335:** Applied on the assumption favorable to Mickens that its relaxed-prejudice route is available; an actual conflict adversely affecting performance remains necessary here, without additional ordinary outcome prejudice.
- **Wood v. Georgia, 450 U.S. 261:** Its inquiry obligation and actual-conflict requirement do not establish the proposed separate exemption from adverse effect in this setting.
- **Strickland v. Washington, 466 U.S. 668:** Ordinary outcome prejudice remains distinct from the adverse-effect requirement under an applicable conflict exception.
- **United States v. Cronic, 466 U.S. 648:** Its exceptional presumed-prejudice circumstances are not established merely by this unperformed inquiry.
- **Swidler & Berlin v. United States:** Post-death confidentiality remains; its survival proves neither a particular confidence nor impairment of this defense.
- **Both Williams v. Taylor decisions:** Constitutional entitlement, concealed-fact cause, factual development and state-merits review remain distinct; the federal hearing does not supply a state merits adjudication.

#### United States v. Emerson, judgment below 270 F.3d 203

**Docket or dockets:** Fifth Circuit No. 99-10331; no Supreme Court docket is established.
**Decided:** 2002-06-17.

For individual arms coverage, §922(g)(8)’s complete order predicates and this protective-adjudication application in United States v. Emerson, Fifth Circuit No. 99-10331, decided June 17, 2002, see Civil Rights — This protective adjudication supports the possession disability.

##### The asserted ignorance-of-law ground does not support dismissal

**Holding:** The Fifth Amendment does not require dismissal of this possession charge for lack of fair warning where the defendant previously signed a federal form specifically identifying the restraining-order disability, later knew of the operative order and its contents, and allegedly continued possessing the firearm more than sixty days after the order. The earlier warning is a concrete circumstance prompting inquiry, not conclusive proof of later recollection or a substitute for the Government's proof of every enacted offense element.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's Part IV, joined by all seven other Associate Justices; eight direct rationale votes. Stone-Zsela agrees only with reversal of this dismissal ground.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The signed purchase-form answer was correct when made before the injunction. This earlier warning and later knowledge of the order distinguish the asserted blanket ignorance-of-law defense; the divorce judge’s failure to add a warning is not dispositive. The ruling does not establish guilt, universal knowledge of the particular law, or satisfaction of any factual-knowledge element.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm reversal of the Fifth Amendment fair-warning dismissal unanimously. The single remaining count is returned for ordinary prosecution; dismissed counts are not revived.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Lambert is distinguished by the specific warning and known operative order; Staples and Bryan retain their offense-specific mental-state rules.

#### United States v. Ruiz, 536 U.S. 622

**Docket or dockets:** No. 01-595.
**Decided:** June 24, 2002.

##### The Constitution does not require advance impeachment disclosure for this plea agreement

**Holding:** The Constitution does not require the Government to provide material impeachment information concerning informants or other witnesses before entering the plea agreement presented here. A competent defendant must actually understand the right relinquished, the charge and the constitutionally relevant consequences and act voluntarily; that requirement does not guarantee advance knowledge of every witness weakness or every fact useful to counsel's assessment.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II of Breyer’s opinion, joined by Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg: seven direct rationale votes.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The agreement’s continuing factual-innocence promise and ordinary plea safeguards form part of the setting, without proof of compliance or an automatic cure. The holding does not decide a supported claim of actual deception, ineffective assistance or an uninformed plea.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Brady v. Maryland, Bagley and Kyles retain their fair-trial disclosure protections, including combined preparation and impeachment uses and case-team responsibility; they do not establish this categorical preagreement timing duty. Strickler’s suppression and retrospective materiality inquiries remain distinct. Brady v. United States permits a knowing voluntary choice despite evidentiary uncertainty. Boykin, Godinez and Bousley preserve actual informed choice, competence and knowledge of the offense. Vonn’s separate judicial warning duties remain, without a finding of forfeiture here.

##### The separate affirmative-defense term is not categorically invalid in this agreement

**Holding:** In the context of the presented agreement, including its continuing promise to disclose information establishing factual innocence, the Constitution does not require advance provision of the identified affirmative-defense information as a prerequisite to the plea bargain. Affirmative-defense information is not equated with impeachment or factual innocence, and the holding does not decide a proved concealed dispositive defense, false representation, factual-innocence suppression, defective advice or an actual uninformed plea.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part III of Breyer’s opinion, joined by Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg: seven direct rationale votes.

**Limits and questions not reached:** A complete affirmative defense may matter despite admitted conduct and may lie outside the factual-innocence promise. The categorical timing claim fails on its merits; absence of a specific withheld item is not an independent holding that an unlawful bargaining condition cannot cause injury.

The sentence-review and operative disposition holdings in United States v. Ruiz, No. 01-595, decided June 24, 2002, appear under Criminal Procedure: Sentencing and Capital Punishment.

#### United States v. Bass, 536 U.S. 862

**Docket or dockets:** No. 01-1471.
**Decided:** June 28, 2002.

##### Armstrong's confined Rule 16 source holding governs this request

**Holding:** Rule 16(a)(1)(C)'s defense-preparation materiality branch does not authorize discovery to prepare this selective-prosecution challenge to the decision to prosecute; Armstrong's holding remains confined to that claim rather than every affirmative defense unrelated to guilt. The distinct intended-use and defendant-origin branches are not established on this record, and protective or sanction powers cannot create an otherwise missing disclosure duty.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of the Court's per curiam opinion has six direct joins: O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg. Stone-Zsela and Breyer join only Parts II–III; Stevens concurs in the judgment on his separate ground.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No alternative discovery source is categorically rejected or established. The following distinct Rule 16 triggers, exclusions and protections retain their own operation.

Rule 16(a)(1)(C) concerns books, papers, documents, photographs, tangible objects, buildings or places within the Government's possession, custody or control. On a defendant's request, inspection and copying or photographing depend on one of three alternatives: materiality to preparation of the defense, intended use by the Government in its evidence in chief, or acquisition from or ownership by the defendant. Those triggers remain distinct.

Paragraph (a)(2) excludes internal attorney or agent reports, memoranda and other Government documents connected with investigation or prosecution, except the disclosures provided by (a)(1)(A), (B), (D) and (E) concerning defendant statements, prior criminal records, examinations and tests, and expert testimony. Government witness statements are separately governed by 18 U.S.C. §3500; no witness-statement production question is resolved here. Paragraph (a)(3) preserves the separate grand-jury disclosure provisions of Rules 6, 12(i), 26.2 and (a)(1)(A). No grand-jury exception is invoked as the source here, and none is decided. These provisions do not establish that every requested aggregate, criterion or deliberative document belongs to the same category.

Rule 16(d)(1) permits protective relief on sufficient showing, including an ex parte written showing whose full text must be sealed and preserved if relief is granted. Paragraph (d)(2) permits discovery, a continuance, exclusion and other just responses to noncompliance. These powers regulate an existing obligation; their existence is not a finding that a duty, exception, privilege or sanction predicate is established here.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Armstrong’s confined defense-preparation construction governs this selection challenge, without excluding every affirmative defense unrelated to guilt. The other two disclosure branches are not established; protective and sanction powers regulate a duty rather than create it.

**Independent alternative holding:** Before obtaining discovery on this indirect racial-selection theory under an available lawful source, a defendant must present some credible evidence tending to show discriminatory effect and purpose, including a reliable inference of different treatment of relevantly similarly situated persons of another race at the challenged federal selection stage; this is less than a prima facie merits case. Grounded statistics, circumstantial comparisons and a specifically justified alternative when comparator names cannot reasonably be obtained may suffice without perfect identity, universal naming or a fixed matching checklist, but Bass's aggregate charging and completed-plea figures lack the required effect comparison.

**Proposition-level authority for alternative holding:** Part II has eight direct joins: Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Stevens agrees that the present order fails through his distinct supported-pattern and bounded-discretion analysis, not by joining this entire rationale.

**Limits, remedy, transition, or precedent treatment:** The effect defect independently defeats the present order; no separate purpose deficiency or ultimate finding of neutrality or discrimination is made. Local capital-eligible charging and national death authorization are distinct stages, and no local-only evidence rule follows. Completed plea figures combine offers and acceptance; Bass received and rejected an offer, without a finding of equal terms. Information asymmetry does not establish a missing comparative inference. Direct discriminatory admissions and whether they dispense with comparators remain undecided. Armstrong, Kimberlin and Klinger preserve attainable, grounded statistical or circumstantial comparisons without perfect identity or universal naming. Wayte and Wade preserve meaningful preliminary screening, not a completed-merits burden. McCleskey requires connection to the challenged decision without excluding statistics; Yick Wo preserves supported pattern inference. Brown v. City of Oneonta’s police-classification rule supplies no missing selection-discovery source or inference.

##### The unsupported order cannot sustain its dependent death-notice sanction

**Holding:** The Sixth Circuit's judgment sustaining the discovery order is reversed, and the refusal-based exclusion of the death notice loses its unsupported predicate and must be set aside. Ordinary pretrial proceedings continue; a later request remains possible only under an available lawful source, a sufficiently grounded preliminary showing and warranted proportionate protective conditions, without any directed new inquiry.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part III of the per curiam opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; eight direct rationale votes. Stevens agrees the dependent sanction falls on his distinct judgment ground.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The proper sanction for refusing a valid discovery order and relief for proved discrimination are not decided. Restoring the ability to proceed with the notice resolves no guilt, capital-eligibility, punishment or execution question.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the judgment sustaining the present order and set aside its dependent death-notice exclusion, 9–0. No evidence collection or hearing is directed; any different later request requires its own lawful source, sufficient foundation and appropriate protections.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Armstrong’s dependent-sanction and ordinary-proceeding principles apply. Carlisle confines supervision to governing legal authority; neither creates a disclosure duty or immunity from constitutional scrutiny.

## Standards and Tests selection: Immigration review channels and protection of former waiver eligibility

### Immigration review channels and protection of former waiver eligibility

**Governing question:** Ordinary judicial review and §212(c) eligibility during the AEDPA/IIRIRA transition.

**Current rule:** From April 1, 1997, corrected §306(c)(1) gives §242(g) cross-temporal reach. Subject to review provided in §242, and notwithstanding other law, the withdrawal concerns claims arising from the Attorney General’s identified decisions or actions to commence proceedings, adjudicate cases or execute removal orders. It does not automatically encompass every legal challenge to a completed adjudication. Former §106 review is not textually provided in new §242; §309 preserves it only for claims outside §242(g)’s specified withdrawal. AEDPA §440(a) independently withdraws the listed criminal-alien ordinary appellate channel even for pending petitions, unless application attaches a supported new legal disability to a protected completed event. Filing, conviction, hardship and review expectations alone do not preserve that channel. Where an alien surrendered a colorable deportability defense before enactment to use the then-available waiver-and-review route, neither §440(d) nor §440(a) clearly withdraws original-claim waiver eligibility or ordinary review on account of that completed choice. That surrendered defense is one protected-event route, not a universal reliance requirement; pleas and eligibility interests are not categorically excluded. Section 440(a) does not clearly repeal independent §2241 jurisdiction. Subject to custody, proper respondent, forum, exhaustion and other applicable predicates, §2241 permits substantial legal or constitutional objections to executive authority to detain or deport, including an unlawful eligibility rule foreclosing discretionary consideration, and effective relief if warranted. Mere reweighing of discretionary equities is distinct, and §1651 supplies no independent jurisdiction.

Section 1252(g)’s named-action withdrawal includes a selection challenge to commencement or adjudication; a constitutional label or potential later final-order petition does not move it outside those acts, and continuing proceedings is not a fourth enumerated act. Nevertheless, §1252(g) does not foreclose existing §1331 jurisdiction over a substantial constitutional selection claim where the claimant has a concrete personal controversy, the actual immigration process cannot adjudicate that claim, and the applicable judicial-review provisions cannot receive its necessary factual record. Constitutional or collateral labeling, disagreement with discretion and preference for earlier review are insufficient. The original basis is §1331 and the existing constitutional action against federal officers, not a jurisdictional grant in §1252(f)(1)’s individual-relief exception. Retained access does not itself establish interim or final relief. Section 1252(f)(2)’s clear-and-convincing condition concerns injunction of removal under a final order, not proceedings without such an order. AEDPA §440(d)’s categorical restriction does not govern a former §212(c) application filed before April 24, 1996 and still awaiting final administrative action at enactment, including a pending administrative appeal. That provision-specific construction requires no surrendered defense, invented plea reliance or vested right to a favorable exercise of discretion. AEDPA §401(e), §440(a) and IIRIRA §309(c)(4)(G) do not clearly repeal the independent §2241 channel for a substantial legal eligibility challenge satisfying its ordinary predicates; permanent §242(b)(9) is not imported wholesale into transitional proceedings. An agency interpretation cannot override the temporal choice established by ordinary statutory construction.

Under the permanent scheme, section 1252(a)(2)(C) bars an ordinary petition challenging legal exclusion from former section 212(c) relief when the alien concedes the covered aggravated-felony removability predicates. The permanent provisions nevertheless retain section 2241 jurisdiction over an independently proper substantial statutory or constitutional objection that executive custody or removal rests on an unlawful eligibility rule. That channel must decide the authority objection and afford effective relief when warranted; it does not authorize general factual retrial or reweighing of lawful discretion. Neither repeal of former section 1105a’s separate habeas provision nor consolidation under section 1252(b)(9) clearly withdraws the independent writ. No indispensable verbal formula or section number is required for a sufficiently clear withdrawal.

Section 1252(c)’s required order attachment and disclosure of earlier judicial validity review apply without themselves granting jurisdiction. Applicable exhaustion and section 1252(d)’s prior-validity-review limitation remain, with the latter’s exceptions for grounds previously unavailable or an inadequate or ineffective earlier remedy. Jurisdictional dismissal of an ordinary petition is not a decision sustaining the order’s merits. Expedited removal under section 1252(e) and the differing discretionary categories under section 1252(a)(2)(B) are not substitutes for the criminal-removability restriction.

Absent sufficiently clear contrary temporal direction, AEDPA section 440(d) and IIRIRA section 304(b)’s repeal do not remove former-law consideration from a lawful permanent resident whose deportable conviction arose from a guilty plea completed before the relevant change while eligible for section 212(c) relief. The retained opportunity does not depend on an official promise, subjective reliance finding or proof that trial would have been chosen. It preserves lawful consideration under the former criteria, not a guaranteed waiver. Those criteria include seven years of lawful unrelinquished domicile and the bar for aggravated-felony conviction with at least five years served for the felony or felonies. Current commencement of removal proceedings selects procedure without itself extinguishing this protected substantive opportunity.

- Reyes-Hernandez v. INS — I. Section 242(g) does not withdraw review of these completed-adjudication claims, 1997-05-12; eight Associates
- Reyes-Hernandez v. INS — II. The two AEDPA restrictions do not attach these new consequences to the completed concession, 1997-05-12; Stevens with Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer; six
- Hincapie-Nieto v. INS — I. The transitional statute does not itself resolve AEDPA's separate restriction, 1997-05-27; eight Associates
- Hincapie-Nieto v. INS — II. Section 440(a) governs this pending ordinary review, 1997-05-27; O'Connor with Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer; six
- Pending-channel limitation and absence of categorical temporal shortcuts: Salazar-Haro, Salazar-Haro v. INS — II. Section 440(a) withdraws this pending ordinary-review channel, Breyer Part II, six direct joins, 1997-06-16; Kolster, Kolster v. INS — II. Section 440(a) governs this ordinary petition, Souter Part II, six direct joins, 1997-06-23.
- Independent habeas construction and its limits: Kolster, Kolster v. INS — III. Independent habeas jurisdiction survives for substantial legal and constitutional objections, Souter Part III, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Ginsburg and Breyer (six), 1997-06-23. 
- Listed criminal grounds and technical correction: IIRIRA §§306(d), 309 and the incorporated text in IIRIRA review and transition provisions, as corrected by Pub. L. 104-302 — Authority, source and exact text; Hincapie-Nieto, Hincapie-Nieto v. INS — II. Section 440(a) governs this pending ordinary review, May 27, 1997; Kolster, Kolster v. INS — III. Independent habeas jurisdiction survives for substantial legal and constitutional objections, June 23, 1997, supplies the distinct surviving collateral channel.

- Named-action coverage and distinct remedy restrictions: Canonical Decision Record `reno_v_american_arab_anti_discrimination_committee_merits_1999-02-24.md`, Public Projection / Holdings / I-A. The withdrawal reaches the named acts in these pending proceedings; I-B. The individual-relief exception does not grant jurisdiction, 1999-02-24; eight direct joins.
- Confined constitutional-access construction: Canonical Decision Record `reno_v_american_arab_anti_discrimination_committee_merits_1999-02-24.md`, Public Projection / Holdings / III-B. A confined construction preserves a capable constitutional forum, 1999-02-24; Souter, Stevens, O’Connor, Ginsburg and Breyer directly join.

- Reno v. Goncalves — Canonical Decision Record, Holdings, “I. The actual statutory habeas channel survives”, 1999-06-03; Stone-Zsela Part I, all nine Justices. Supplies channel-specific statutory preservation.
- Reno v. Goncalves — Canonical Decision Record, Holdings, “II. Section 440(d) does not govern this pending application”, 1999-06-03; six-Justice full-opinion coalition. Parts II–III supply the pending-application construction and rejection of contrary Soriano treatment.

- Permanent ordinary-review restriction, retained habeas and effective-relief conditions: Canonical Decision Record `Calcano_Martinez_v_INS_merits_2001-06-25.md`, Public Projection / Holdings / I. The permanent criminal-removability limitation bars these ordinary petitions; II. Independent habeas remains available for a substantial legal eligibility objection; III. The direct dismissals do not adjudicate the separate habeas actions, 2001-06-25; Part I unanimous; Parts II–III have six direct joins.
- Then-eligible prechange guilty pleas, surviving former conditions and lawful-consideration remedy: Canonical Decision Record `INS_v_St_Cyr_merits_2001-06-25.md`, Public Projection / Holdings / II. The 1996 changes do not eliminate eligibility attached to this completed plea; III. St. Cyr receives consideration under the former law, not the waiver itself, 2001-06-25; seven direct joins to Stevens’s Parts II–III; Part I unanimously applies Calcano’s retained channel.

**Present operation effective:** Calcano-Martinez v. INS and INS v. St. Cyr, 2001-06-25, for permanent-scheme channels and then-eligible prechange guilty pleas, respectively; earlier transitional, pending-application and constitutional-access components retain their identified effective dates.


**Exceptions and limits:** An actual surrendered defense is necessary to the Reyes-Hernandez lost-option rationale, not to every possible retroactivity claim. Pleas and eligibility for discretionary relief are not categorically excluded as protected events. Mere filing, conviction, hardship or review expectations alone do not preserve the ordinary §440(a) channel; Goncalves’s pending-application eligibility construction rests on the distinct enacted structure of §440(d). No exemption for every preenactment conviction, pending proceeding or later reopening follows. Eligibility does not compel favorable discretion.

**Open question:** The independent Suspension Clause minimum, consequences of clear elimination of all effective legality review, and general factual or discretionary review remain unresolved. St. Cyr decides neither trial convictions, pleas after the relevant change, persons ineligible when pleading nor unrelated later removal grounds. Calcano decides no disputed alienage or offense-classification case and no underlying constitutional merits of its petitioners’ claims. Recognizing jurisdiction supplies no other petitioner’s custody, respondent, forum, exhaustion or relief predicates. AADC’s independent constitutional minimum remains open where the retained statutory construction does not apply or an adequate later route exists.


**Related current rules:** IIRIRA commencement and preserved review channels states the enacted commencement, differing discretionary lists, agency-election conditions and completed-judgment savings.

**Controlling construction:** Corrected §440(a) identifies the following final-order category; its withdrawal is subject to the channel and temporal constructions above:

> (10) Any final order of deportation against an alien who is deportable by reason of having committed a criminal offense covered in section 241(a)(2) (A)(iii), (B), (C), or (D), or any offense covered by section 241(a)(2)(A)(ii) for which both predicate offenses are, without regard to the date of their commission, otherwise covered by section 241(a)(2)(A)(i), shall not be subject to review by any court.

Section 440(d) separately removes §212(c) eligibility for the same enumerated §241(a)(2) categories. IIRIRA §306(d) supplies the identical commission-date qualification in both dual-offense branches, effective as if included in AEDPA. The exact subsection references govern; these decisions do not reconstruct every underlying criminal definition or establish that every old conviction is protected.
